Proof of two-dimensional Jacobian conjecture
Yucai Su Department of Mathematics, Jimei University, Xiamen, 361021, China, and School of Mathematical Science, Tongji University, Shanghai, 200092, China yucaisu@jmu.edu.cn

Abstract
. Using the local bijectivity of Keller maps, we give a proof of two-dimensional Jacobian conjecture .



1.
Main theorem

Let
(F,G)\in{\mathbb{C}}[x,y]^{2}
be a pair of two polynomials. The Jacobian problem is to give necessary and sufficient conditions that

{\mathbb{C}}[F,G]={\mathbb{C}}[x,y]
. In geometric terms if

\sigma:\mathbb{C}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{2},\ p\mapsto(F(p),G(p)):=(F(a,b),G(a,b))
for
p=(a,b)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

(1.1)

then the necessary and sufficient condition is that
\sigma
be invertible.

By the chain rule an obvious necessary condition is that the Jacobian determinant is a nonzero constant

J(F,G):={\rm det\,}\Big[{}^{\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}}_{\frac{\partial G}{\partial x}}\,\,{}^{\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}}_{\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}}\Big]\in\mathbb{C}_{\neq 0}

.

If this condition is verified we call
(F,G)
a
Jacobian pair
and the corresponding map
\sigma
the
Keller map
.
The aim of this paper is to give a proof of the well known two-dimensional Jacobian conjecture (cf.
[
2
,
6
]
), formulated by Ott-Heinrich Keller in 1939:

Jacobian conjecture
(cf.
[
2
,
6
]
): If
(F,G)
is a Jacobian pair then the Keller map
\sigma

is bijective, i.e.,

F,G
are generators of
\mathbb{C}[x,y]
.

It is a well known and standard fact that, in order to prove this conjecture, it is enough to prove that the map
\sigma

is injective. This is what we do in

Theorem 1.1
.

The Keller map
\sigma

is injective. Consequently, the
2
-dimensional Jacobian conjecture holds.

We prove this theorem by contradiction, so we can start with the converse assumption that there exists a Jacobian pair
(F,G)
such that the corresponding Keller map
\sigma
is not injective, i.e., there exist
p_{{1}}=(x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),

p_{{2}}=(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
with

\sigma(p_{{1}})=\sigma(p_{{2}})
,
p_{{1}}\neq p_{{2}}
.

(1.2)

For convenience, we denote

(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}^{4}
, and

\displaystyle V=\big\{(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in\mathbb{C}^{4}\,\big|\,\sigma(p_{{1}})=\sigma(p_{{2}}),\,p_{{1}}\neq p_{{2}}\big\}.

(1.3)

By assumption (
1.2
),
V\neq\emptyset
(which, to be proven in Lemma
2.20
, is in fact
a smooth and closed algebraic surface
in
\mathbb{C}^{4}
).
We define the
height
(or the
norm
) of
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in V
to be

{h}_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}=|x_{{1}}|+|y_{{1}}|+|x_{{2}}|+|y_{{2}}|.

(1.4)

To prove Theorem
1.1
, we need two results.
Here is the first one.

Theorem 1.2
.

There exists some automorphism
\phi
of
\mathbb{C}[x,y]
such that the Jacobian pair
\big(\phi(F),\phi(G)\big)
, which for convenience is still denoted by
(F,G)
, satisfies
the following: for
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})\in V

when
{h}_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}\to\infty
,
we have
{|y_{{1}}|+|y_{{2}}|=o({h}_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}).}

The reason that the result in this theorem is not symmetric on
x,y
is because we require
(F,G)
to satisfy
(
2.2
), where the variables
x
and
y
are not symmetric.

Once we have established Theorem
1.2
, we fix the Jacobian pair
(F,G)
and the variety
V
satisfying this theorem. Then we define the projection
\pi_{1}:V\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
by

\pi_{1}:(p_{1},p_{2})\ \ \mapsto\ \ \pi_{1}(p_{1},p_{2}):=(x_{1},x_{2})
for
(p_{1},p_{2})=\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)\in V
.

(1.5)

Then the second result can be stated as follows.

Theorem 1.3
.

The projection
\pi_{1}
is proper, finite and surjective.

We will give the proofs of the above two theorems in section
2
.
Then finally in section
3
, by a rather technical study of the properties so far developed of the variety
V
, and with no further use of the fact that
V
arises from the Jacobian problem, we find a contradiction to Theorem
1.3
thus proving the Jacobian conjecture.

1.3
and
Proposition
3.12
.

2.
Proofs of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3

2.1.
Some preparations

For any ring
R
, we use
R((y^{-1}))
to denote the ring whose elements have the form
\sum_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}a_{i}y^{i}
with
a_{i}\in R
such that
a_{i}=0
when
i\gg 1
.
By
R[[y^{-1}]]
we denote the set of usual power series
\sum_{i=-\infty}^{0}a_{i}y^{i}
with
a_{i}\in R
. Then
R((y^{-1}))
is the (algebraic) localization
R((y^{-1}))=R[[y^{-1}]][y]
.

Remark 2.1
.

(i)

An element
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}y^{-i}
is invertible in
R[[y^{-1}]]
if and only if
a_{0}
is invertible in
R
.
An element in
R((y^{-1}))
can be uniquely written as
y^{k}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}y^{-i}
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a_{0}\neq 0
and it is invertible in
R((y^{-1}))
if and only if
a_{0}
is invertible in
R
.

(ii)

Given
f:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}y^{-i}
we can define in
R[[y^{-1}]]
the
substitution homomorphism

y^{-1}\mapsto f
.
If moreover
f
is invertible in
R((y^{-1}))
this extends to a substitution in
R((y^{-1}))
.

Recall the following standard formula, which holds algebraically in the ring
\mathbb{C}[[z]]
and converges absolutely when
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|z|<1
,

(1+z)^{\beta}=\Big(1+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}{\displaystyle\binom{\beta}{j}}z^{j}\Big),\displaystyle\mbox{ \ where \ }\beta\in\mathbb{R},\ \ \binom{\beta}{j}:=\frac{\beta(\beta-1)\cdots(\beta-(j-1))}{j!},

(2.1)

and in general, we denote the
multi-nomial coefficient

{\binom{k}{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{i}}=\frac{k(k-1)\cdots(k-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\cdots+\lambda_{i}-1))}{\lambda_{1}!\lambda_{2}!\cdots\lambda_{i}!}.}

Remark 2.2
.

(i)

If
A
is a commutative ring, two formal series
f(z),g(z)\in A[[z]]
can be composed as if they were functions by

f\circ g(z)=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}$}a_{j}\Big(\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}$}b_{i}z^{i}\Big)^{j}\mbox{ \ if $f(z)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}z^{i},\ g(z)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}b_{i}z^{i}.$}

(2.2)

Moreover
f(z)
is invertible with respect to
\circ
if and only if
a_{1}
is invertible in
A
. We will denote by
f^{\circ-1}
its inverse in order to avoid confusions.

(ii)

In particular if
f
is invertible we can find
h(z)
so that
g(z)=f\circ h(z)
[or
g(z)=h\circ f(z)\scriptscriptstyle\,
].

(iii)

Finally the usual
chain rule
holds.

We need some conventions and notations, which, for easy reference, are listed as follows.

Convention 2.3
.

(1)

For
a\in\mathbb{C}
, we write
a=a_{\rm re}+a_{\rm im}{\textbf{{i}}}
for some

a_{\rm re},a_{\rm im}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
{\textbf{{i}}}=\sqrt{-1}
.
If
a^{b}
appears somewhere, then we always assume
b\in\mathbb{R}
, and in case
a\neq 0
, we
interpret
a^{b}
as the unique complex number
r^{b}e^{b{\theta}{\textbf{{i}}}}
by writing

a=re^{{\theta}{\textbf{{i}}}}
for some
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
0\leq{\theta}<2\pi
.

(2)

Let
P=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}p_{i}y^{\alpha-i}=y^{\alpha}\big(1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}p_{i}y^{-i}\big)\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z},\,p_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
and
p_{0}=1
.

(i)

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{Q}
with
\alpha\beta\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then we can uniquely define
P^{\beta}
to be an element in
\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
as follows,

\displaystyle P^{\beta}=y^{\alpha\beta}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}$}{\displaystyle\binom{\beta}{j}}\Big(\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}$}p_{i}y^{-i}\Big)^{j}\Big)\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1})),

\displaystyle\Big(\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}$}p_{i}y^{-i}\Big)^{j}=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\sum\lambda_{i}=j}$}{\displaystyle\binom{j}{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...}}p_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}p_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}p_{3}^{\lambda_{3}}\cdots y^{-\sum i\lambda_{i}}.

(2.3)

(ii)

For
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
, we use
P(Q_{1},Q_{2})
and
P|_{(x,y)=(Q_{1},Q_{2})}
to denote the following element [as long as it is algebraically
a well-defined element in
\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1})){\scriptstyle\,}
],

P(Q_{1},Q_{2})=P|_{(x,y)=(Q_{1},Q_{2})}=\sum\limits_{i}p_{i}(Q_{1})Q_{2}^{\alpha-i}.

(2.4)

(iii)

If
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, we also use (
2.4
) to denote a well-defined complex number as long as
the series (
2.4
) converges absolutely
\!{\scriptstyle\!}.

(iv)

Assume
\alpha\neq 0
.
For any
Q=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}q_{i}y^{\alpha_{1}-i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
with
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
q_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
, by comparing coefficients of
y^{\alpha_{1}-i}
in (
2.5
) for
i\geq 0
, there exists uniquely
b_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
such that
we can algebraically write

Q=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}$}b_{i}P^{\frac{\alpha_{1}-i}{\alpha}}.

(2.5)

We call
b_{i}
the
coefficient of
P^{\frac{\alpha_{1}-i}{\alpha}}
in
Q
,
and denote by
{\rm C_{oeff}}(Q,P^{\frac{\alpha_{1}-i}{\alpha}})
. We also use the following notation
{\rm C_{oeff}}(Q,x^{i}y^{j})
:

\phantom{mmmMMM}{\rm C_{oeff}}(Q,x^{i}y^{j})=q_{ij}\mbox{ \ \ if \ $Q$ can be written as $Q=\sum\limits_{i,j}q_{ij}x^{i}y^{j}$ with $q_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}$.}

(2.6)

(3)

Let
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}\to 0
be a variable. Sometimes we need to consider elements in
\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
which may depend on
\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}
.
For any
P\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
(or especially in
\mathbb{C}
) which depends on
\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}
, if

P(a,b)
converges absolutely and
|{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}^{-i}P(a,b)|<{\textbf{{s}}}
for some
i\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
and some fixed

{\textbf{{s}}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, where
(a,b)
is in some required region,
then we use
O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{i}
to denote
P
:

P=O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{i}.

(2.7)

If
a,b
are some variables depending on another variable
c\to c_{0}
(for some
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
) such that

\lim_{c\to c_{0}}\frac{a}{b}=0
,
then we also denote

\displaystyle a=o(b).

(2.8)

Let
P=\sum_{j}p_{j}y^{j}\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1})),\,p_{j}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
and
(x_{0},y_{0})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
If
z_{0}=\sum_{j}|p_{j}(x_{0})y_{0}^{j}|
converges (in particular this requires that
y_{0}\neq 0
if
p_{j}(x_{0})\neq 0
for some
j<0
),
then
z_{0}
is denoted by
A_{(x_{0},y_{0})}(P)
[or by
A_{(y_{0})}(P)
if
P
is independent of
x
].

Definition 2.4
.

(1)

Let
P
be as above and

Q=\sum_{i}q_{i}y^{i}\in\mathbb{C}((y^{-1})),\,q_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
. If
|p_{i}(x_{0})|\leq q_{i}

for all possible

i
,
then we say
Q
is a
controlling function
for
P
on

y
at point
x_{0}
, and denote

P\,\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,Q
or
Q\,\unrhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,P

,

(2.9)

or
P\,\unlhd_{y}\,Q
when there is no confusion.
In particular if
P,Q
are independent of
y
then we write
P\unlhd^{x_{0}}Q
(thus
a\unlhd b
for
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
simply means that
|a|\leq b
with
b\geq 0
).

(2)

An element in
\mathbb{C}((y^{-1}))
with non-negative coefficients (such as
Q
above)
is called a
controlling function
on
y
.

(3)

If
Q=q_{0}y^{\alpha}+\sum_{j>0}q_{j}y^{\alpha-j}\in\mathbb{C}((y^{-1}))
is a controlling function on

y
with
q_{0}>0
, then we always use the same symbol with
subscripts “
ign
” and
“
neg
” to denote the elements

\displaystyle Q_{\rm ign}=q_{0}^{-1}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j>0}$}q_{j}y^{-j},

\displaystyle Q_{\rm neg}:=q_{0}y^{\alpha}\Big(1-q_{0}^{-1}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j>0}$}q_{j}y^{-j}\Big)=q_{0}y^{\alpha}(1-Q_{\rm ign})=2q_{0}y^{\alpha}-Q.\!\!\!\!\!\!

(2.10)

We call
Q_{\rm ign}
the
ignored part
of
Q
, and
Q_{\rm neg}
the
negative correspondence of

Q

[in sense of (
(1)
), where
a,-k
are nonpositive].

Notice that when
Q
in (
2.11
) is a controlling function then for
k\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we have that both
\big(1\!-\!q_{0}^{-1}\sum_{j>0}q_{j}y^{-j}\big)^{-k}
and
\frac{(q_{0}y^{\alpha})^{k}}{1-kQ_{\rm ign}}
are controlling functions.

Lemma 2.5
.

(1)

If

P=p_{0}y^{\alpha}+\sum\limits_{j>0}p_{j}y^{\alpha-j}\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1})),\ \ \ Q=q_{0}y^{\alpha}+\sum\limits_{j>0}q_{j}y^{\alpha-j}\in\mathbb{C}((y^{-1})),

(2.11)

with
P\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}Q
,
x_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
and
|p_{0}(x_{0})|=q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
then for
a,b,k\in\mathbb{Q}
with
a\alpha,b\alpha,k\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }\displaystyle\frac{\partial P}{\partial y}\ \unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\ \,\pm{\scriptstyle\,}\frac{dQ}{dy},\ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle{\rm(b)\ }P^{a}\ \unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\ \,Q_{\rm neg}^{a}\ \unlhd_{y}\ \,(q_{0}y^{\alpha})^{-b}Q_{\rm neg}^{a+b}\mbox{ for $a,b\in\mathbb{Q}_{-}$,}

\displaystyle{\rm(c)\ }Q^{k}\ \unlhd_{y}\ \,(q_{0}y^{\alpha})^{2k}Q_{\rm neg}^{-k}\ \unlhd_{y}\ \,\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{(q_{0}y^{\alpha})^{k}}{1-kQ_{\rm ign}}&\mbox{if }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\\[6.0pt]
\displaystyle(q_{0}y^{\alpha})^{k}\Big(1+\displaystyle\frac{kQ_{\rm ign}}{1-Q_{\rm ign}}\Big)&\mbox{if }k\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\mbox{ with }k<1.\end{array}\right.

where
\eqref{MAMS1}\,(a)
holds under the condition: either both
P,

Q
are polynomials of

y

(
in this case the sign is “
+
”
{\scriptscriptstyle\,})
, or else both are power series of
y^{-1}

(
in this case the sign is “
-
”
{\scriptscriptstyle\,})
.

(2)

If
x_{0},y_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
and
P_{1}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,Q_{1},\ P_{2}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,Q_{2}
, then

A_{(x_{0},y_{0})}(P_{1}P_{2})\leq A_{(y_{0})}(Q_{1})A_{(y_{0})}(Q_{2})=Q_{1}(|y_{0}|)Q_{2}(|y_{0}|).

(2.15)

Proof.
One can see that (2)
and (
(1)
) (a) are obvious, and (
(1)
) (b), (c) are obtained by
noting that for
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}_{-}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, one has

\displaystyle(-1)^{i}\binom{a}{i}=\Big|\binom{a}{i}\Big|{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}\Big|\binom{a+b}{i}\Big|{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}(-1)^{i}\binom{a+b}{i}
,

\displaystyle\binom{k}{i}{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}\Big|\binom{-k}{i}\Big|{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}k^{i}&\mbox{if }k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\\[4.0pt]
k&\mbox{if }0<k\in\mathbb{Q}_{<1}.\end{array}\right.

This proves the lemma.
\Box

Throughout the rest of this section, for convenience we regard
y^{-1}
as the variable
z
whenever necessary.

Take, where
\tilde{f}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
with
\tilde{f}_{1}\in\mathbb{C}_{\neq 0},

\displaystyle\tilde{F}=\tilde{f}_{1}y^{-1}+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=2}^{\infty}$}\tilde{f}_{i}y^{-i}\in\mathbb{C}[x][[y^{-1}]].

(2.19)

Then
\tilde{F}
is invertible under the composition “
\circ
” defined in
(
2.2
) (by regarding
y^{-1}
as
z
). We refer
\tilde{F}^{\circ-1}
to as the
formal inverse function
of
\tilde{F}
. Then
\tilde{F}^{\circ-1}(y^{-1})\in\mathbb{C}[x][[y^{-1}]]
, and thus we can write

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\circ-1}(y^{-1})={\textbf{{b}}}_{1}y^{-1}+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=2}^{\infty}$}{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}y^{-i}\ \ \mbox{ for some }\ \ {\textbf{{b}}}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x].

(2.20)

By definition, we have (noting that
y^{-1}
is the identity element under the composition “
\circ
”),

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!y^{-1}

\displaystyle\!\!=\!\!

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\circ-1}\circ\tilde{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{comp}}}}{{=}}\tilde{F}^{\circ-1}(\tilde{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{Inv-of-T-F}}}}{{=}}{\textbf{{b}}}_{1}\tilde{F}+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=2}^{\infty}$}{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}{\tilde{F}}^{i}.

(2.21)

By the notation in Convention
2.3
(2) (iv), we see from (
2.21
) that

{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}={\rm C_{oeff}}(y^{-1},\tilde{F}^{i})\in\mathbb{C}[x]
for
i\geq 1
,

(2.22)

which can be precisely determined by using (
2.19
) to substitute
\tilde{F}
in (
2.21
) and comparing the coefficients of
y^{-i}
in both sides of (
2.21
) such that
(we do not need to use the following explicit expression of
{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}
, but we only want to present that
{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}
’s exist),

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\textbf{{b}}}_{1}

\displaystyle\!\!=\!\!

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{1}^{-1}\in\mathbb{C}_{\neq 0},

(2.23)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}

\displaystyle\!\!=\!\!

\displaystyle-\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{i-1}$}{\textbf{{b}}}_{j}\tilde{f}_{1}^{j-i}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{j}$}{\displaystyle\binom{j}{\ell}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{n}\geq 0}}{{{}^{\ }_{\scriptstyle\lambda_{1}+2\lambda_{2}+\cdots+n\lambda_{n}={\scriptscriptstyle\,}i-j}}}}$}{\displaystyle\binom{\ell}{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{n}}}\tilde{f}_{1}^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\cdots-\lambda_{n}}\tilde{f}_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\tilde{f}_{3}^{\lambda_{3}}\cdots\tilde{f}_{n}^{\lambda_{n}},\ i\geq 2.\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\hat{a}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\hat{a}_{1}>0
, and let

{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}=\hat{a}_{1}y^{-1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{\infty}\hat{a}_{i}y^{-i}\in\mathbb{C}[[y^{-1}]]\mbox{ \ \ and \ ${\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P---}}}}{{=}}\hat{a}_{1}y^{-1}-\sum\limits_{i=2}^{\infty}\hat{a}_{i}y^{-i}\in\mathbb{C}[[y^{-1}]]$},

(2.24)

be a controlling function on
y
and its negative correspondence.
Write the formal inverse function
{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}
of
{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}
, as

{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}(y^{-1})=\hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{1}y^{-1}+\sum\limits_{i=2}^{\infty}\hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}y^{-i}\ \ \mbox{ for some }\ \ \hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}.

(2.25)

Then
\hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{1}=\hat{a}_{1}^{-1}
and

(1)

{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}(y^{-1})
is a controlling function on
y^{-1}

(
this means that the formal inverse function of the
negative correspondence of a controlling function is a controlling function
)
,
i.e., for
i\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}\geq 0
.

(2.26)

(2)

If
\tilde{F}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}
with
\tilde{F}
as in (
2.19
)
and
|\tilde{f}_{1}|=\hat{a}_{1}
,
then by regarding
\tilde{F}
as a variable, we have the following
(
this means that the formal inverse function of a function can be controlled by the formal inverse function of the negative correspondence of a controlling function that controls the said function
)
,

\displaystyle y^{-1}=\tilde{F}^{\circ-1}(\tilde{F})\unlhd_{{\tilde{F}}}^{x_{0}}\,{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}(\tilde{F}),\mbox{ \ i.e.,}

\displaystyle{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{bbi==}}}}{{=}}{\rm C_{oeff}}(y^{-1},\tilde{F}^{i})\ \unlhd^{x_{0}}\ \hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}={\rm C_{oeff}}(y^{-1},{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}^{i}_{\rm neg})\mbox{ \ for \ }i\geq 2,

(2.27)

where
{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}\unlhd^{x_{0}}\,\hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}
means that

|{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}(x_{0})|\leq\hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}
.
In particular

y^{-1}\unlhd_{y}\,{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}({\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}),

(2.28)

where the right side of “
\unlhd_{y}
” is regarded as a function of
y
by using (
2.24
) to substitute
{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}
.

Proof.
Note that (1) is a special case of (2) by taking, in (
2.19
),
\tilde{f}_{1}=\hat{a}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{i}=0
for
i\geq 2
, i.e.,
\tilde{F}=\hat{a}_{1}y^{-1}
[then by definition
\tilde{F}^{\circ-1}(y^{-1})
is

\hat{a}_{1}^{-1}y^{-1}
, i.e.,
{\textbf{{b}}}_{1}=\hat{a}_{1}^{-1}
and
{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}=0
if
i\geq 2
, and so (
2.26
) follows from (
(2)
) ].

Thus we prove (2).
Note from (
2.20
) that
{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}
is the coefficient of
y^{-i}
in the formal inverse function of
\tilde{F}
, or equivalently, the coefficient of
\tilde{F}^{i}
in
y^{-1}
when we regard
y^{-1}
as a function of
\tilde{F}
by (
2.21
).
Thus
i!{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}
is the constant term of the
i
-th partial derivative
\frac{\partial^{i}y^{-1}}{{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\,}}\partial{\tilde{F}}^{i}}
.
Therefore to prove (
(2)
), first we want to prove, for
i\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{i}y^{-1}}{{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\,}}\partial{\tilde{F}}^{i}}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,\frac{d^{i}y^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}{}_{\rm neg}^{i}}{\scriptscriptstyle\,},

(2.29)

where the left-hand side is understood as that we first use (
2.21
) to
regard
y^{-1}
as a function of
\tilde{F}
(with parameter
x
) and
apply
\frac{\partial^{i}}{{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle\,}}\partial{\tilde{F}}^{i}}
to it, then regard the result as a function
of
y^{-1}
by using (
2.19
) to substitute
\tilde{F}
(and the like for the right-hand side, which does not contain the parameter
x
).
By (
(1)
) (a), we have

\frac{\partial\tilde{F}}{\partial y^{-1}}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,\frac{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}}{dy^{-1}}
(here we regard
y^{-1}
as a variable), and thus by
(
(1)
) (b) and definition (
(3)
),

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\partial\tilde{F}}{\partial y^{-1}}\Big)^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm{\eqref{MAMS1}\,(b)}}}}{{\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}}}\,\Big(\Big(\frac{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}}{dy^{-1}}\Big)_{\rm neg}\Big)^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm{\eqref{P---}}}}}{{=}}\Big(\frac{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}}{dy^{-1}}\Big)^{-1},

(2.30)

i.e.,
\frac{\partial y^{-1}}{\partial\tilde{F}}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,\frac{dy^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}}
and (
2.29
) holds for
i=1
. Inductively, by Lemma
2.5
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{i}y^{-1}}{\partial\tilde{F}^{i}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{F}}\Big(\frac{\partial^{i-1}y^{-1}}{\partial\tilde{F}^{i-1}}\Big)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{-1}}\Big(\frac{\partial^{i-1}y^{-1}}{\partial\tilde{F}^{i-1}}\Big)\Big({\frac{\partial\tilde{F}}{\partial y^{-1}}}\Big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{\frac{\partial^{i}y^{-1}}{\partial\tilde{F}^{i}}=}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm inductive\ assumption\ and\ \eqref{FiQQQ}}}}{{\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}}}\frac{d}{dy^{-1}}\Big(\frac{d^{i-1}y^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}{}_{\rm neg}^{i-1}}\Big)\Big({\frac{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}}{dy^{-1}}}\Big)^{-1}=\frac{d^{i}y^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}{}_{\rm neg}^{i}}\,.

(2.31)

This proves (
2.29
). Using (
2.29
) and noting from (
2.21
), (
2.25
),
we have (noting that
\frac{d^{i}y^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}{}_{\rm neg}^{i}}\Big|_{y^{-1}=0}
should be understood as
the constant term in
\frac{d^{i}y^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}{}_{\rm neg}^{i}}
when it is written as a series of
y^{-1}
),

\begin{array}[]{llll}\displaystyle{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}&\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{i-Faa}}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}y^{-1}}{\partial\tilde{F}^{i}}\Big|_{\tilde{F}=0}=\frac{1}{i!}\frac{\partial^{i}y^{-1}}{\partial\tilde{F}^{i}}\Big|_{y^{-1}=0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{to0000}}}}{{\unlhd^{x_{0}}}}\,\frac{1}{i!}\frac{d^{i}y^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}{}_{\rm neg}^{i}}\Big|_{y^{-1}=0}=\frac{1}{i!}\frac{d^{i}y^{-1}}{d{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}{}_{\rm neg}^{i}}\Big|_{{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}=0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ha--f-}}}}{{=}}\hat{\textbf{{b}}}_{i}.\end{array}\!\!\!\!

(2.32)

This proves (
(2)
).

Note that for any controlling functions
Q,P_{2}
, and any
P_{1}\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
with
P_{1}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}P_{2}
, we always have
Q(P_{1})\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}Q(P_{2})
. This
with the facts that
\tilde{F}\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}
and that
{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}
is a controlling function implies

{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}(\tilde{F})\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}\,{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}({\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}})
.

(2.33)

This together with (
(2)
)
proves

y^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{HAHAHAHHJ}}}}{{\unlhd_{{\tilde{F}}}^{x_{0}}}}\,{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}(\tilde{F})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ff-thattt-that}}}}{{\unlhd^{x_{0}}_{y}}}\,{\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}({\,\hat{\textbf{{$\!$F$\scriptscriptstyle\,$}}}}),

(2.34)

i.e., we have
(
2.28
).
\Box

2.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

First we need to reformulate
(F,G
) (by applying some automorphism of
\mathbb{C}[x,y]
).
Fix a sufficiently large
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Applying the following
variable change,

(x,y)\mapsto(y,\,y^{\ell}+x),

(2.35)

and rescaling
F,G
, we can assume that
F,G
have the following forms,
for some
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
f_{j,k},g_{j,k}\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }F{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}y^{m}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}F_{1},\ \ \ F_{1}{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}\Big(\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m-1-j}f_{j,k}x^{k}\Big)y^{j},$}

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }G{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}y^{n}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}G_{1},\ \ \ G_{1}{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}\Big(\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1-j}g_{j,k}x^{k}\Big)y^{j}.$}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(2.36)

Note that
{\rm deg}\,F_{1}\leq m-1,\,{\rm deg}\,G_{1}\leq n-1
(where
{\rm deg}\,F_{1}
denotes the total degree of
F_{1}
).
For simplicity, we can also assume
2\leq n
and
n{\scriptscriptstyle\,}|{\scriptscriptstyle\,}m
(i.e.,
\frac{m}{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
)
by replacing
(F,G)
by
\big(F+(G+F^{k})^{k},G+F^{k}\big)
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}

[the reason we assume
n|m
will be clear in (
2.64
), (
2.2
) and (
2.78
), after all it is a reasonable choice].

Thus the new pair
(F,G)
is in fact obtained from the original one by applying some automorphism of
\mathbb{C}[x,y]
and a change of generators in
\mathbb{C}[F,G]
.

In the following we consider
F,G
as elements in the ring
\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
.

By (
2.2
), we can rewrite
F,G
as, where
f_{i}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}f_{m-i,k}x^{k}
,
g_{i}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{i-1}g_{n-i,k}x^{k}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
,
and where in general for any
A\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
we use
{\rm deg}_{x}A
, called the
x
-degree
of
A
, to denote the degree of
A
with respect to variable
x
,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }F=y^{m}(1+y^{-m}F_{1})=y^{m}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}$}\Big(\mbox{$\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m-1-j}$}f_{j,k}x^{k}\Big)y^{j-m}\Big)=y^{m}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}$}f_{i}(x)y^{-i}\Big),\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }G=y^{n}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=0}^{n-1}$}\Big(\mbox{$\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n-1-j}$}g_{j,k}x^{k}\Big)y^{j-n}\Big)=y^{n}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}$}g_{i}(x)y^{-i}\Big),

\displaystyle{\rm(iii)\ }{\rm deg}_{x}f_{i}\leq i-1\mbox{ if }i\leq m\mbox{ and }f_{i}=0\mbox{ if }i>m,\ \

\displaystyle{\rm(iv)\ }{\rm deg}_{x}g_{i}\leq i-1\mbox{ if }i\leq n\mbox{ and }g_{i}=0\mbox{ if }i>n.

(2.37)

At this point we want to define a choice of an
m
-th
root for
F
denoted
F^{\frac{1}{m}}
by using formulas (
2.1
) and
(
(i)
),

F^{\frac{1}{m}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P-as}}}}{{:=}}\ y\Big(1+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(x)y^{-i}\Big)^{\frac{1}{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{bimeformo}}}}{{=}}y\Big(1+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}{\displaystyle\binom{\frac{1}{m}}{j}}\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(x)y^{-i}\Big)^{j}\Big).

(2.38)

This is not just an algebraic formula but setting

||F_{1}||:=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}\Big(\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m-1-j}|f_{j,k}x^{k}|\Big)|y|^{j},

(2.39)

it converges absolutely when

||F_{1}||<|y|^{m}.

Set
f_{0}=g_{0}=1
, and
denote the set
A=\frac{n-\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}{m}
.
By (
2.2
), as in (
2.5
) and (
2.21
),
we can algebraically write,

\displaystyle G=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}c_{\alpha}F^{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{\frac{n-i}{m}}F^{\frac{n-i}{m}}\mbox{ \ for some $c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}[x]$},$}

(2.40)

where as in (
2.23
), by comparing the coefficients of
y^{n-i}
, we can inductively determine
c_{\frac{n-i}{m}}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
for
i\geq 0
as follows (again we do not need the explicit expression of
c_{\frac{n-i}{m}}
):

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!c_{\frac{n-i}{m}}

\displaystyle\!\!=\!\!

\displaystyle g_{i}-\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{i-1}$}c_{\frac{n-j}{m}}f_{1}^{j-i}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{j}$}{\displaystyle\binom{j}{\ell}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{n}\geq 0}}{{{}^{\ }_{\scriptstyle\lambda_{1}+2\lambda_{2}+\cdots+n\lambda_{n}={\scriptscriptstyle\,}i-j}}}}$}{\displaystyle\binom{\ell}{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},...,\lambda_{n}}}f_{1}^{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\cdots-\lambda_{n}}f_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}f_{3}^{\lambda_{3}}\cdots f_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}.

Remark 2.7
.

(i)

If
U=1+\mbox{$\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}$}u_{i}y^{-i}\in\mathbb{C}[x][[y^{-1}]]
with
u_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x],\,{\rm deg}_{x}u_{i}\leq i
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
then
U^{\beta}=1+\mbox{$\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}$}v_{i}y^{-i}
for some
v_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]

with
{\rm deg}_{x}v_{i}\leq i
.

(ii)

Furthermore, if for all
i
we have
{\rm deg}_{x}u_{i}<i
we have
{\rm deg}_{x}v_{i}<i
.

Proof.
This follows from a symbolic computation. Think first the
u_{i}
as variables of weight
i
then,
using formula (
2.1
) it is enough to prove that, for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
we have that in
\big(\mbox{$\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}$}u_{i}y^{-i}\big)^{j}
it is a polynomial
in
y^{-1}
where the coefficient of
y^{-a}
is a polynomial in the
u_{i}
of weight
a
. This is immediate by writing
u_{i}y^{-i}=(\lambda^{i}u_{i})(\lambda y)^{-i}
with
\lambda
an auxiliary parameter.

Now by hypothesis the degree of
u_{i}
is smaller than or equal to
its weight and this property is preserved, the same if it is always smaller than its weight.
\Box

Lemma 2.8
.

We have, for some
a_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }c_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}\mbox{ if }\alpha>\frac{-m+1}{m},\ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }c_{-1}=0,\ \ \ \ \ {\rm(iii)\ }{\rm deg}_{x}c_{\frac{-m+1-j}{m}}\leq j+1\mbox{ if }j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

\displaystyle{\rm(iv)\ }c_{\frac{-m+1}{m}}=-\frac{J_{0}}{m}(x+a_{1}).

(2.41)

Proof.
First by (
2.40
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}{=}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\alpha c_{\alpha}F^{\alpha-1}\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}.

(2.42)

Then by (
2.40
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial G}{\partial x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-2}}}}{{=}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}F^{\alpha}+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\alpha c_{\alpha}F^{\alpha-1}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{pat-G-y}}}}{{=}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}F^{\alpha}+\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}\Big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\Big)^{-1}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}.

(2.43)

Equivalently,
\frac{\partial G}{\partial x}\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}=\mbox{$\sum_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}F^{\alpha}\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}+\frac{\partial G}{\partial y}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle-J_{0}=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}F^{\alpha}\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}.

(2.44)

This gives (i) below, then multiplying (
2.40
) with
\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}
gives (ii) below, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }J_{0}\Big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\Big)^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{G-toF}}}}{{=}}-\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}F^{\alpha},\ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }G\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-2}}}}{{=}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\Big(\mbox{$\sum\limits_{-1\neq\alpha\in A}$}\frac{c_{\alpha}F^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\Big)+c_{-1}F^{-1}\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}.

(2.45)

The
y
-degree of the left-hand side of (
2.45
) (i) is
-m+1
, while that of the right-hand side is
m\alpha_{0}
, where
\alpha_{0}\in A
is the maximal number with
\frac{dc_{\alpha_{0}}}{dx}\neq 0
.
We obtain (
2.8
) (i).

Since, for some
\beta_{i},\gamma_{i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
,

{\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wheraraaa+1}\,(i)}}}{{=}}my^{m-1}\Big(1+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\beta_{i}y^{-i}\Big)\implies\Big({\displaystyle\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}}\Big)^{-1}={\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}}y^{-m+1}\Big(1+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{i}y^{-i}\Big),

(2.46)

with

(1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{i}y^{-i})=(1+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\beta_{i}y^{-i})^{-1}
,
comparing coefficients of
y^{-m+1}
in (
2.45
) (i) gives that
\frac{dc_{\alpha_{0}}}{dx}=-m^{-1}J_{0}
with
\alpha_{0}=\frac{-m+1}{m}
, which implies (
2.8
)(iv)
for some
a_{1}\in\mathbb{C}
.

By comparing coefficients of
y^{-1}
in (
2.45
) (ii), we obtain (
2.8
) (ii).

By (
2.2
) (iii), (iv), we in particular have

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }{\rm deg}_{x}{\rm C_{oeff}}(F,y^{m-j})\leq j,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm deg}_{x}{\rm C_{oeff}}(G,y^{n-j})\leq j,

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }{\rm deg}_{x}{\rm C_{oeff}}\mbox{\large$\Big($}\frac{\partial F}{\partial y},y^{m-1-j}\mbox{\large$\Big)$}={\rm deg}_{x}{\rm C_{oeff}}(F,y^{m-j})\leq j\mbox{ \ \ for \ }j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

(2.47)

Then by Remark
2.7
we have:

\displaystyle{\rm deg}_{x}{\rm C_{oeff}}\mbox{\large$\Big($}\Big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\Big)^{-1},y^{-m+1-j}\mbox{\large$\Big)$}\leq j,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm deg}_{x}{\rm C_{oeff}}(F^{\frac{n-j}{m}},y^{n-j-k})\leq k.

(2.48)

From this and (
2.45
) (i), we claim that (
2.8
) (iii) holds by induction on
j
by comparing the coefficients of
y^{-m+1-j}
.

To prove the claim, from Remark
2.7
and formula (
2.38
) one has for any
a\in\mathbb{Z}
,

F^{\frac{a}{m}}=y^{a}\Big(1+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}\,\ell_{i,a}(x)y^{-i}\Big)\quad\mbox{ for some }\ell_{i,a}(x)\in\mathbb{C}[x]\mbox{ with }{\rm deg}_{x}\ell_{i,a}(x)\leq i.

(2.49)

By (
2.46
), formula (
2.45
) (i) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{J_{0}}{m}y^{-m+1}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}$}\gamma_{i}y^{-i}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\rm\eqref{ome-3}\,(i),\,\eqref{root2}}}}{{=}}-\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}y^{m\alpha}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}$}\ell_{i,m\alpha}(x)y^{-i}\Big)

\displaystyle\phantom{\frac{J_{0}}{m}y^{-m+1}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}$}b_{i}y^{-i}\Big)}\ \ \ \ \ \ =\ \ \ -\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}\Big(y^{m\alpha}+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\infty}$}\ell_{i,m\alpha}(x)y^{m\alpha-i}\Big).

(2.50)

The coefficient of
y^{-m+1-j}
of the left-hand side of the previous formula is
\frac{J_{0}}{m}\gamma_{j}
if
j\geq 1
of degree
\leq j
, it is
\frac{J_{0}}{m}
for
j=0
. Instead, that of the right-hand side of the previous formula is
-\frac{d}{dx}c_{\frac{-m-(j-1)}{m}}
plus the sum for
\alpha,i\geq 1
with
m\alpha-i=-m+1-j
[or
m\alpha=-m-(j-i-1)\scriptscriptstyle\,
] of
-\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}\ell_{i,m\alpha}(x)
. By what we have proved only
\alpha\leq\frac{-m+1}{m}
contribute that is
-(j-i-1)\leq 1
or
i\leq j
.
By induction the degree of
-\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}
is
\leq j-i
so these terms contribute to a degree
\leq j=(j-i)+i
and the claim follows.
The basis for the induction is for
j=0
for which we have (
2.8
) (iv) degree 1.
\Box

We would like to mention that the following lemma [which in particular implies (
2.106
) ] is important for us to obtain that the
m
-th root
\omega^{\prime}
of unity appearing in the proof of Lemma
2.17
is equal to
1
[cf. (
2.72
) (ii) and the arguments after (
2.2
) ].

Lemma 2.9
.

By some reformulation of
(F,G)
, we may

assume

\displaystyle c_{\frac{4-m}{m}}c_{\frac{3-m}{m}}\neq 0.

(2.51)

Proof.
Fix any
a_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
with
\alpha_{0}\neq 3a_{1}
. We define
\bar{F},\bar{G}
below (then
\bar{F},\bar{G}
are still polynomials giving a counterexample to the Jacobian conjecture)
such that
\bar{F}
have the form (i) below, for some
\bar{f}_{j,k}\in\mathbb{C}

,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\bar{F}:=F(y,y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wheraraaa}\,(i)}}}{{=}}(y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x)^{m}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}\Big(\sum\limits_{k=0}^{m-1-j}f_{j,k}y^{k}\Big)(y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x)^{j}$}

\displaystyle\phantom{{\rm(i)\ }\bar{F}}=y^{\bar{m}}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\bar{m}}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{j-1}$}\bar{f}_{j,k}y^{-j}x^{k}\Big),

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }\bar{G}=G(y,y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x),\!\!\!

(2.52)

where we have used the same symbol with a bar to denote an associated element corresponding to the Jacobian pair
(\bar{F},\bar{G}
); in particular,
\bar{n}=3n,\,\bar{m}=3m
.

When
(x,y)
is set to
(y,y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x)=\big(y,y^{3}(1+a_{0}y^{-1}-xy^{-3})\big)
, we have that
y^{-1}
is set to
y^{-3}(1+a_{0}y^{-1}-xy^{-3})^{-1}=y^{-3}(1-a_{0}y^{-1}+\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}t_{j}(x)y^{-j})
for some
t_{j}(x)\in\mathbb{C}[x]
. By Remark
2.7
,
{\rm deg}_{x}t_{j}<j
.

So for
H\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
the

substitution

H\mapsto H|_{(x,y)=(y,y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x)}:=\phi(H)
,

(2.53)

is a well-defined homomorphism which we denote for simplicity by
\phi
.
Note from (
2.2
) (i) or (
2.2
) (i) that
\bar{F}=\phi(F)
has the form,

\displaystyle\bar{F}=\phi(F)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wheraraaa+1}\,(i),\,\eqref{ome-6}\,(i)}}}{{=}}(y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x)^{m}+\mbox{(terms with $y$-degree $\leq 3m-2)$}

\displaystyle\phantom{\bar{F}}=y^{\bar{m}}+ma_{0}y^{\bar{m}-1}+\mbox{(terms with $y$-degree $\leq 3m-2)$}.

(2.54)

In particular, since
\phi(F^{\frac{-m-i}{m}})
is an
m
-th root of
\phi(F)^{-m-i}
, we
have

\displaystyle\phi(F^{\frac{-m-i}{m}})=\phi(F)^{\frac{-m-i}{m}}=\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}-3i}{\bar{m}}}.

(2.55)

Thus, we have the following, where the omitted terms are terms with lower
y
-degrees, or with lower powers of
\phi(F)
,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\phi(F)^{\frac{1}{\bar{m}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P-as},\,\eqref{form-barF}}}}{{=}}y+\frac{a_{0}}{3}+\cdots\implies\phi(F)^{\frac{h}{\bar{m}}}=y^{h}+\frac{a_{0}}{3}hy^{h-1}+\cdots,\ \mbox{ \ for $h\in\mathbb{Z}$, \ \ thus, }

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }y^{h}=\phi(F)^{\frac{h}{\bar{m}}}-\frac{a_{0}}{3}h\phi(F)^{\frac{h-1}{\bar{m}}}+\cdots\ \ \in\mbox{$\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}$}\mathbb{C}[x]\bar{F}^{\frac{h-k}{\bar{m}}},

\displaystyle{\rm(iii)\ }\phi\Big(c_{\frac{-m-i}{m}}F^{\frac{-m-i}{m}}\Big)=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}$}p_{k,i}(x)\phi(F)^{\frac{2-2i-k-\bar{m}}{\bar{m}}}\quad\text{for some $p_{k,i}\in\mathbb{C}[x]$ and }\ i\geq-1,

(2.56)

where, by the fact in (
2.8
) (iii) that
c_{\frac{-m-i}{m}}
for
i\geq-1
has
x
-degree
\leq i+2
, we see that
\phi(c_{\frac{-m-i}{m}})
has
y
-degree
\leq i+2
, and so (iii) has
y
-degree
\leq i+2-3(m+i)=2-2i-\bar{m}
, and thus we obtain (iii) from (ii).

Note that one can exchange the substitution with the series in the definition of

\displaystyle\bar{G}:=\phi(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-2}}}{{=}}\phi\Big(\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}$}c_{\frac{n-i}{m}}F^{\frac{n-i}{m}}\Big)=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}$}\phi\Big(c_{\frac{n-i}{m}}F^{\frac{n-i}{m}}\Big)=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}$}\phi\Big(c_{\frac{-\bar{m}+(\bar{n}+\bar{m}-3i)}{\bar{m}}}F^{\frac{-\bar{m}+(\bar{n}+\bar{m}-3i)}{\bar{m}}}\Big)\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\phantom{\bar{G}}=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{-(\bar{n}+\bar{m})\leq i<\infty,\,i\in 3\mathbb{Z}}$}\phi\Big(c_{\frac{-\bar{m}+i}{\bar{m}}}F^{\frac{-\bar{m}-i}{\bar{m}}}\Big).

(2.57)

Write the right-hand side of (
2.2
) as

\mbox{ r.h.s.~of \eqref{MEMEMR--}}\ \ =\ \ \sum\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}\bar{c}_{\frac{\bar{n}-i}{\bar{m}}}\bar{F}^{\frac{\bar{n}-i}{\bar{m}}}\mbox{ \ for some \ $\bar{c}_{\frac{\bar{n}-i}{\bar{m}}}\in\mathbb{C}[x]$.}

(2.58)

Then of course we have the bar version of Lemma
2.8
; in particular,
\bar{c}_{\frac{-\bar{m}+i}{\bar{m}}}\in\mathbb{C}
for
i\geq 2
by (
2.8
) (i).

Claim 2.10
.

Only the term with
i=-3
in the right-hand side of (
2.2
) can contribute to

\bar{c}_{\frac{-\bar{m}+3}{\bar{m}}},\bar{c}_{\frac{-\bar{m}+4}{\bar{m}}}
.

This can be proven as follows. If
i\leq-6
then
\frac{-\bar{m}-i}{\bar{m}}>\frac{1-m}{m}
and
c_{\frac{-\bar{m}-i}{\bar{m}}}
is a constant by (
2.8
) (i) and thus the correspondent term is equal to
c_{\frac{-\bar{m}-i}{\bar{m}}}\bar{F}^{\frac{-\bar{m}-i}{\bar{m}}}
, which cannot contribute;
if
i=0
the term is zero by (
2.8
) (ii); if
i\geq 3
then by (
2.2
) (iii) the term has
y
-degree
\leq 2-\frac{2i}{3}-\bar{m}<-\bar{m}+3
, which cannot contribute. Thus the claim follows.

Now consider the term with
i=-3
in the right-hand side of (
2.2
). We prove

\displaystyle\phi\Big(c_{\frac{-m+1}{m}}F^{\frac{-m+1}{m}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-5}\,(iv),\,\eqref{phhhi}}}}}{{=}}-\frac{J_{0}}{m}(y+a_{1})\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}+3}{\bar{m}}}

\displaystyle\phantom{\phi\Big(c_{\frac{-m+1}{m}}F^{\frac{-m+1}{m}}\Big)\ \,\ \ \ \ \ \,}\in\ \ \,\ -\frac{J_{0}}{m}\Big(\phi(F)^{\frac{4-\bar{m}}{\bar{m}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}\big(a_{1}{\scriptscriptstyle\!}-{\scriptscriptstyle\!}\frac{a_{0}}{3}\big)\phi(F)^{\frac{3-\bar{m}}{\bar{m}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}$}\mathbb{C}[x]\phi(F)^{\frac{2-\bar{m}-j}{\bar{m}}}\Big).\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(2.59)

In fact,

\!\!\!\!y\,\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}+3}{\bar{m}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{that-y}\,(i)}}}{{=}}y^{-\bar{m}+4}+(-\bar{m}+3)\frac{a_{0}}{3}y^{-\bar{m}+3}+T_{1}\ \mbox{ for some }\ T_{1}\in\mbox{$\sum\limits_{k=-2}^{\infty}$}\mathbb{C}[x]\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}-k}{\bar{m}}}.\!\!\!\!

(2.60)

From formula (
2.2
) we deduce

\displaystyle y^{-\bar{m}+4}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{that-y}\,(ii)}}}{{=}}\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}+4}{\bar{m}}}-(-\bar{m}+4)\frac{a_{0}}{3}\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}+3}{\bar{m}}}+T_{2}\ \ \mbox{ for some }\ \ T_{2}\in\mbox{$\sum\limits_{k=-2}^{\infty}$}\mathbb{C}[x]\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}-k}{\bar{m}}},\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle y^{-\bar{m}+3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{that-y}\,(ii)}}}{{=}}\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}+3}{\bar{m}}}+T_{3}\ \ \mbox{ for some }\ \ T_{3}\in\mbox{$\sum\limits_{k=-2}^{\infty}$}\mathbb{C}[x]\phi(F)^{\frac{-\bar{m}-k}{\bar{m}}}.

(2.61)

Thus the part “
\in
” in (
2.2
) follows from (
2.60
), (
2.2
), i.e., we have (
2.2
).

By Claim
2.10
and (
2.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{\frac{-\bar{m}+4}{\bar{m}}}=-\frac{J_{0}}{m}\neq 0,\ \ \ \ \ \ \bar{c}_{\frac{-\bar{m}+3}{\bar{m}}}=-\frac{J_{0}}{m}(a_{1}-\frac{a_{0}}{3})\neq 0.

(2.62)

Thus by replacing
(F,G)
by
(\bar{F},\bar{G})
[by (
2.2
) we still have (
2.2
) after the replacement], we have the lemma.
\Box

From now on, we fix, once and for all, the Jacobian pair
(F,G)
satisfying (
2.2
) and Lemma
2.9

[and obviously,
(F,G)
is obtained from the original Jacobian pair by applying some automorphism of
\mathbb{C}[x,y]\scriptscriptstyle\,
].

In the rest of this section we regard all elements as in the ring

\displaystyle{\mathbf{R}}=\mathbb{C}[x^{\frac{1}{m}}]((y^{-1})),

(2.63)

where
x^{\frac{1}{m}}
is regarded as a parameter such that its
m
-th power is
x
.

Now, to be more precise, we slightly generalize notions and notations in Definition
2.4
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
R=\sum_{i,j}r_{ij}x^{i}y^{j},\,Q=\sum_{i,j}q_{ij}x^{i}y^{j}\in{\mathbf{R}}
with
r_{ij}\in\mathbb{C},\,q_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

(i)

If
|r_{ij}|\leq q_{ij}
for all possible
i,j
,
then we say
R
is controlled by
Q
with respect to
x,y
, and
denote

R\unlhd_{x,y}Q
.

(ii)

For
x_{0},y_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
, if
\sum_{i,j}|r_{ij}x_{0}^{i}y_{0}^{j}|
converges, then we say the series
R

with respect to
x,y

converges
strongly
when
(x,y)
is set to
(x_{0},y_{0})
, and denote
R|_{(x,y)=(x_{0},y_{0})}=\sum_{i,j}r_{ij}x_{0}^{i}y_{0}^{j}
.

Note that
Lemmas
2.5
and
2.6
can be parallelly generalized.

Denote

\displaystyle t=(1+x^{\frac{1}{m}})^{m-1}\in{\mathbf{R}}.

(2.64)

Throughout the rest of this section,
we always use
{\textbf{{s}}}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
to denote some fixed number for all possible
j
; for instance, we can take
{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}=\sum_{j,k}|f_{j,k}|
in (
2.2
).

By (
2.2
) (iii), we can observe below that every term
x^{a}y^{b}
appearing in
F
(with non-negative integral
a,b
) also appears in
{\textbf{{F}}}_{1}
by using (
2.64
) to expand
t^{j}
in
{\textbf{{F}}}_{1}
,
thus we obtain,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{denote-t}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,y}}}{\textbf{{F}}}_{1}:=y^{m}\Big(1+{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}$}\big(ty^{-1}\big)^{j}\Big)\unlhd_{x,y}{\textbf{{F}}}:=y^{m}\Big(1+{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}$}\big(ty^{-1}\big)^{j}\Big),\mbox{ \ where \ }

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }{\textbf{{F}}}=y^{m}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}ty^{-1}}{1-ty^{-1}}\Big).

(2.65)

Remark 2.12
.

The importance of (
2.2
) is that though the structure of the polynomial
F
may be complicated (and in particular we do not have any information about the coefficient of
x^{i}y^{j}
in
F
for general
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
), we can always use the controlling function
F
, which has the very simple form in (
2.2
) (ii), to control
F
; consequently, we are able to choose the simple controlling function
P
in (
2.2
) to control
P
, which allows us to obtain the simple form of the formal inverse function of
{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}
in (
2.74
); then we can conveniently use Lemma
2.6
to obtain Lemma
2.14
, which is the key to obtain Lemma
2.17
.

Now by (
2.2
) (ii) and definition (
(3)
) we have

\displaystyle{\textbf{{F}}}_{\rm ign}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P---}}}}{{=}}\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}ty^{-1}}{1-ty^{-1}}.

(2.66)

Thus by Lemma
2.5
, for
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle F^{\pm\frac{j}{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{MAMS1}\,(b),\,(c)}}}{{\unlhd_{x,y}}}y^{\pm j}\Big(1-{\textbf{{F}}}_{\rm ign}\Big)^{-\frac{j}{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{F-ign---}}}}{{=}}y^{\pm j}\Big(\frac{1-ty^{-1}}{1-(1+{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})ty^{-1}}\Big)^{\frac{j}{m}}.

(2.67)

We use (
(i)
) to define

\displaystyle P=F^{-\frac{1}{m}},

(2.68)

which can be written as the form in (i) below for some
p_{j}\in\mathbb{C}[x]
, then we
use (
2.67
) and Lemma
2.5
to obtain,

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }P{\scriptscriptstyle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P==defineP}}}}{{:=}}{\scriptscriptstyle}F^{-\frac{1}{m}}=y^{-1}\Big(1+\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{\infty}$}p_{j}y^{-j}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{S-2-D}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,y}}}\frac{y^{-1}}{(1-{\textbf{{F}}}_{\rm ign})^{\frac{1}{m}}}{\scriptstyle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{MAMS1}\,(b)}}}{{\unlhd_{x,y}}}{\scriptstyle}{\textbf{{P}}},\mbox{ \ where,}

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }{\textbf{{P}}}{\scriptscriptstyle}:={\scriptscriptstyle}\frac{y^{-1}}{1{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}{\textbf{{F}}}_{\rm ign}}{\scriptscriptstyle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{F-ign---}}}}{{=}}{\scriptscriptstyle}\frac{y^{-1}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}ty^{-1})}{1{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})ty^{-1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}y^{-1}\Big(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}ty^{-1}}{1{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})ty^{-1}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P---}}}}{{=}}{\scriptscriptstyle}y^{-1}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm ign})\mbox{ with}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle{\rm(iii)\ }{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm ign}{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}ty^{-1}}{1{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})ty^{-1}}.

(2.69)

Thus by definition (
(3)
),

\displaystyle{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}=y^{-1}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm ign})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P=Sm1}\,(iii)}}}{{=}}y^{-1}\Big(1-\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}ty^{-1}}{1{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})ty^{-1}}\Big)=\frac{y^{-1}\big(1-(1+2{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})ty^{-1}\big)}{1-(1+{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})ty^{-1}}.

(2.70)

For convenience, we denote

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{j}:=c_{-\frac{j}{m}}=\tilde{c}_{{0},j}+\tilde{c}_{{1},j}\mbox{ with }\tilde{c}_{{0},j}\in\mathbb{C},\ \tilde{c}_{{1},j}\in x\mathbb{C}[x]\mbox{ for all possible }j.

(2.71)

Then by (
2.8
) and Lemma
2.9
,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\tilde{c}_{{1},j}=0\mbox{ \ for \ }j\leq m-2,\ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }\tilde{c}_{m-4}\tilde{c}_{m-3}\neq 0.

(2.72)

Notation 2.13
.

We denote
\hat{c}_{{1},j}\in x\mathbb{R}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}}[x]
to be the polynomial of
x
which is obtained from
\tilde{c}_{{1},j}\in x\mathbb{C}[x]
by replacing the coefficient

{\rm C_{oeff}}(\tilde{c}_{{1},j},x^{k})
by its absolute value for all possible
j,k
.

Lemma 2.14
.

We have, for some
{\textbf{{s}}}_{1},{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }G=G_{0}+G_{1}\mbox{ \ with}

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }G_{0}:=G|_{(x,P)=(0,P)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-2},\,\eqref{Mememdnbrnrn}}}}{{=}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\tilde{c}_{{0},j}P^{j}

\displaystyle\phantom{{\rm(ii)\ }G_{0}}\unlhd_{x,P}P^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}P}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}P}\Big)\unlhd_{x,y}{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}{\textbf{{P}}}}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}{\textbf{{P}}}}\Big),\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle{\rm(iii)\ }G_{1}:=G-G_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-2},\,\eqref{Mememdnbrnrn}}}}{{=}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\tilde{c}_{{1},j}P^{j}

\displaystyle\phantom{{\rm(iii)\ }G_{1}}\unlhd_{x,P}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=m-1}^{\infty}$}x\frac{d\hat{c}_{{1},j}}{dx}P^{j}\unlhd_{x,P}J_{0}m^{-1}xP^{m-1}\Big(1-\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}tP}\Big)^{-1}.

(2.73)

Proof.
Solving
y^{-1}
from (
2.70
) we obtain that the formal inverse function of

, which by notations in (

), (

[noting that if (

then we have two solutions and obviously the other solution, when expanded as a series of

, contains a nonzero constant term and thus is not the one we require],

\displaystyle\displaystyle y^{-1}={\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}({\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}):=\frac{1+(1+{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}-B}{2(1+2{\textbf{{s}}}_{0})t},\ \ B=(1-\beta_{+}t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg})^{\frac{1}{2}}(1-\beta_{-}t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg})^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.74)

where we always regard an element
(1+a)^{\frac{1}{2}}
as the unique element defined by the formula (

.

Regarding

), we obtain, where the second “
\unlhd_{x,{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}}
” is obtained by the fact that

,

\displaystyle B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{MAMS1}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}}}}(1-\beta_{+}t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg})^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-\beta_{-}t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\unlhd_{x,{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}}(1-\beta_{+}t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg})^{-1}.

(2.75)

Using this, we can deduce from
(

) the following,

\displaystyle{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}({\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P=Sm1+1},\,\eqref{ThatAADS}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}}}}{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{3}t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{4}t{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}}\Big)\mbox{ for some }{\textbf{{s}}}_{3},{\textbf{{s}}}_{4}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

(2.76)

Thus by (
(1)
), (

), we have, by choosing sufficiently large

,

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }y^{-1}\ \ \ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{HAHAHAHHJ}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,P}}}\ \ {\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}^{\circ-1}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{W-negg}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,P}}}P\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{3}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{4}tP}\Big)\unlhd_{x,P}P\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}tP}\Big),

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }y\ \,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P=Sm1+2}\,(i),\,\eqref{MAMS1}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,P}}}\ \,P^{-1}\Big(1-\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{3}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{4}tP}\Big)^{-1}\unlhd_{x,P}P^{-1}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}tP}\Big),

(2.77)

where the second “

), and (ii) is obtained from the first two “

By (

) and (

\displaystyle G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P=Sm1+2},\eqref{wheraraaa}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,P}}}P^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{6}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{5}tP}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{MAMS1},\,\eqref{P=Sm1}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,y}}}{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{6}t{\textbf{{P}}}}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{5}t{\textbf{{P}}}}\Big).

From this, (

\displaystyle G_{0}=G|_{(x,P)=(0,P)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{TDSP}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,P}}}P^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{6}(t|_{x=0})P}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{5}(t|_{x=0})P}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{TDSP}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,y}}}{\textbf{{P}}}_{\rm neg}^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{6}(t|_{x=0}){\textbf{{P}}}}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{5}(t|_{x=0}){\textbf{{P}}}}\Big),

i.e., we have (

By (

\displaystyle m^{-1}(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y})\unlhd_{x,y}y^{m-1}\Big(1+{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m-1}$}\big(ty^{-1}\big)^{j}\Big).

Thus we have,

\displaystyle\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\frac{d\tilde{c}_{{1}j}}{dx}P^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P==defineP},\,\eqref{Mememdnbrnrn}}}}{{=}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{\alpha\in A}$}\frac{dc_{\alpha}}{dx}F^{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-3}\,(i)}}}{{=}}-J_{0}\Big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\Big)^{-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\frac{d\tilde{c}_{{1}j}}{dx}P^{j}}\ \ \ \ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P=Sm1+2}}}}{{\unlhd_{x,P}}}\ \ \ J_{0}m^{-1}y^{-(m-1)}\Big(1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{0}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m-1}$}(ty^{-1})^{j}\Big)^{-1}\Big|_{y^{-1}=P(1+\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{3}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{4}tP})}

\displaystyle\phantom{\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\frac{d\tilde{c}_{{1}j}}{dx}P^{j}}\ \ \ \ \ \unlhd_{x,P}\ \ \ J_{0}m^{-1}P^{m-1}\Big(1-\frac{{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}tP}{1-{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}tP}\Big)^{-1},

(2.81)

where the first “
\unlhd_{x,P}
” is obtained from (
2.2
) (i) and the second is obtained by choosing sufficiently large
{\textbf{{s}}}_{1},{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}
.
Now the first “
\unlhd_{x,P}
” of (
2.14
) (iii) is obvious and the second “
\unlhd_{x,P}
” is obtained from (
2.2
).
This proves the lemma.
\Box

The following proposition is a stronger version of Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 2.15
.

There exists
{\textbf{{s}}}_{7}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

such that for any
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in V
with

{{h}_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}\geq{\textbf{{s}}}_{7},}

we must have

|y_{{1}}|<{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ |y_{{2}}|<{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}.

(2.82)

We prove this proposition by contradiction and in several steps. Assume thus that the statement leading to formula (
2.82
) does not hold.

Step 1.
In this step we prove that, under the previous hypotheses, either for this Jacobian pair
(F,G)
, there exists
(p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i})

=\big((x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i}),(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})\big)\in V

for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
satisfying,

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm(i)\ }{{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}\geq\frac{1}{2}i,}\ \ \ x_{{{1}},i}\neq x_{{{2}},i},\mbox{ \ \ \ }{\rm(ii)\ }|y_{{{1}},i}|\geq\frac{1}{2}{h}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}},\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(2.83)

or
for an equivalent Jacobian pair
(\tilde{F},\tilde{G})

obtained by a simple linear change of coordinates and still satisfying Lemma
2.9
, the tilde version of (
2.83
) holds.

Proof of Step 1.
Assume (
2.82
) does not hold, i.e., there exists
(p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i})

=\big((x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i}),(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})\big)\in V

for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
satisfying

{{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}\geq i,}

such that

{\rm(i)\ \ }|y_{{{1}},i}|\geq{h}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}},\ \ \ \mbox{or}\ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ \ }|y_{{{2}},i}|\geq{h}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}}.

(2.84)

Since at least one of the conditions in (
2.84
) must hold for infinitely many
i
’s, if necessary by switching
p_{{{1}},i}
and
p_{{{2}},i}
, we can assume (
2.84
) (i) holds for infinitely many
i
.
If necessary
by replacing the sequence by a subsequence
[if the sequence
(p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i})
is replaced by the subsequence
(p_{{{1}},{i_{j}}},p_{{{2}},{i_{j}}})
, then we always have
i_{j}\geq j
; thus we still have
{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}\geq i
after the replacement],
we may assume (
2.84
) (i) holds for all
i
. Finally if for infinitely many indices
i
we have
{x_{{{1}},i}\neq x_{{{2}},i}}
still passing to a subsequence we may assume that
{x_{{{1}},i}\neq x_{{{2}},i}}
holds for all
i
and the given pair
(F,G)
satisfies the conditions of Step 1.

Thus we may assume that
{x_{{{1}},i}=x_{{{2}},i}}
holds for all
i
.
Take the equivalent Jacobian pair

\displaystyle(\tilde{F},\tilde{G})=\Big(F\big(x+\frac{y}{2},y\big),G\big(x+\frac{y}{2},y\big)\Big).

(2.85)

which corresponds to the change of coordinates

\displaystyle(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\big(x-\frac{y}{2},y\big).

(2.86)

Note that our
(F,G)
in (
2.85
) was obtained, in Lemma
2.9
by the initial choice of
(F,G)
defined in (
2.2
), and the coordinate change
(x,y)\mapsto(y,y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-x)
therefore, when we change

to
x+\frac{y}{2}
in (
2.85
),
it amounts to that the tilde version of
(F,G)
is obtained from the initial
(F,G)
by mapping
(x,y)\mapsto(y,y^{3}+a_{0}y^{2}-\frac{y}{2}-x)
.
Then one can observe that for the tilde version of
(F,G)
, we
still have the second equality of (
2.2
) and thus we still have
(
2.2
), Claim
2.10
and (
2.62
).
Hence, the proof of Lemma
2.9
works again for
(\tilde{F},\tilde{G})
.
This proves that all results before Proposition
2.15
hold for the tilde version Jacobian pair
(\tilde{F},\tilde{G})
.

Denote the points
(p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i})
in the new coordinates as
(\tilde{p}_{1,i},\tilde{p}_{2,i})
below for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, then (
2.85
) together with the fact that
(p_{1,i},p_{2,i})\in V
shows that it is in
\tilde{V}

(which is the tilde version of

), i.e.,

\displaystyle(\tilde{p}_{1,i},\tilde{p}_{2,i})=\big((\tilde{x}_{1,i},\tilde{y}_{1,i}),(\tilde{x}_{2,i},\tilde{y}_{2,i})\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{Change-varr}}}}{{=}}\Big(\big(x_{1,i}-\frac{y_{1,i}}{2},y_{1,i}\big),\big(x_{2,i}-\frac{y_{2,i}}{2},y_{2,i}\big)\Big)\in\tilde{V}.

(2.87)

Since by construction
p_{{{1}},i}\neq p_{{{2}},i}
and the assumption
x_{1,i}=x_{2,i}
we have thus
\tilde{x}_{1,i}\neq\tilde{x}_{2,i}
for all
i
.

Note that every inequality below [except the second inequality in (ii), which follows from (i) ] is obtained from one of the following three facts: (1) formula
(
2.84
) (i) hold and
h_{p_{1,i},p_{2,i}}\geq i
by noting from arguments after (
2.84
);
(2)
h_{p_{1,i},p_{2,i}}=|x_{1,i}|+|y_{1,i}|+|x_{2,i}|+|y_{2,i}|
by definition (

); (3)
\pm(|a|-|b|)\leq|a-b|\leq|a|+|b|
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
, therefore we have

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\frac{i}{2}\leq\frac{h_{p_{1,i},p_{2,i}}}{2}\leq|x_{1,i}|+\frac{|y_{1,i}|}{2}+|x_{2,i}|+\frac{|y_{2,i}|}{2}\leq\Big|x_{1,i}-\frac{y_{1,i}}{2}\Big|+|y_{1,i}|+\Big|x_{2,i}-\frac{y_{2,i}}{2}\Big|+|y_{2,i}|

\displaystyle\phantom{{\rm(i)\ }\frac{i}{2}}=\tilde{h}_{\tilde{p}_{1,i},\tilde{p}_{2,i}}\leq|x_{1,i}|+\frac{3|y_{1,i}|}{2}+|x_{2,i}|+\frac{3|y_{2,i}|}{2}\leq 2h_{p_{1,i},p_{2,i}},

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }|\tilde{y}_{1,i}|=|y_{1,i}|\geq{h}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{EveryIn}\,(i)}}}{{\geq}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\tilde{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle\tilde{p}_{1,i},\tilde{p}_{2,i}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\tilde{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle\tilde{p}_{1,i},\tilde{p}_{2,i}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}.

(2.88)

This proves that the tilde version of (
2.83
) holds.

For the purpose of proving Proposition
2.15
, to simplify notations, in case the tilde version of (

) holds, we re-denote

as
(F,G)
until the end of the proof of Proposition

[note that we abuse notations

,

{\rm(i)\ }h_{p_{1,i},p_{2,i}}\geq\frac{1}{2}i,\ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }|y_{{{1}},i}|\geq\frac{1}{2}{h}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}},\ \ \ {\rm(iii)\ }x_{1,i}\neq x_{2,i}.

(2.89)

Step 2.
In this tep we prove that, for the previous sequence of points, we have formula (
2.94
) that is

\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{y_{{{2}},i}}{y_{{{1}},i}}=\omega,\ \ \mbox{where $\omega$ is some $m$-th root of unity.}

Notation 2.16
.

Before continuing our proof, we need to use the following notations: Let
a,b,c
be variables such that
c\to\infty
or
c\to 0
and
a,b
are functions of
c
, we denote

{\rm(i)\ }a\sim_{c\scriptscriptstyle\,}b,\ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }a\prec_{c\scriptscriptstyle\,}b,\ \ \ \ {\rm(iii)\ }a\preceq_{c\scriptscriptstyle\,}b
,

(2.90)

which mean respectively, for some fixed
{\textbf{{s}}}_{1},{\textbf{{s}}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}\leq\Big|\frac{a}{b}\Big|\leq{\textbf{{s}}}_{2},\ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }\lim\limits_{c\to\infty}\frac{a}{b}=0,\ \ \ \ {\rm(iii)\ }\Big|\frac{a}{b}\Big|\leq{\textbf{{s}}}_{1}
.

(2.91)

Then obviously, properties (i), (ii), (iii) are respectively an equivalence, a strict order, an order.

Now for
k={1},{2}
, since
|x_{k,i}|\leq{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}},\,|y_{k,i}|\leq{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}
,
by (
2.2
) (i), we have, where (ii) is obtained from (i) and (
2.2
) (i),

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }F_{1}(x_{k,i},y_{k,i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wheraraaa}\,(i)}}}{{\preceq_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}^{m-1}\prec_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m^{2}}{m+1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{mqp1234-5+}\,(ii)}}}{{\preceq_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}|y_{{{1}},i}|^{m},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }F(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})\sim_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}y^{m}_{{{1}},i}.

(2.92)

Thus, where the first equality follows from the fact that
\sigma(p_{1,i})=\sigma(p_{2,i})
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle 1=\frac{F(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})}{F(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})}=\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{F(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})}{F(x_{{1},i},y_{{1},i})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wheraraaa}}}}{{=}}\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{\frac{y^{m}_{{{2}},i}}{y^{m}_{{{1}},i}}+\frac{F_{1}(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})}{y^{m}_{{{1}},i}}}{1+\frac{F_{1}(x_{{1},i},y_{{1},i})}{y^{m}_{{{1}},i}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{S1pq}}}}{{=}}\lim_{i\to\infty}\Big(\frac{y_{{{2}},i}}{y_{{{1}},i}}\Big)^{m}.

(2.93)

Therefore, by replacing the sequence by a subsequence, we have [we would like to remark that
\omega
defined below is not necessarily equal to
1
, but we will prove that the
m
-th root
\omega^{\prime}
of unity defined in (

) must be
1
],

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{y_{{{2}},i}}{y_{{{1}},i}}=\omega,\ \ \mbox{where $\omega$ is some $m$-th root of unity.}

(2.94)

Step 3.
In this step we show that the series appearing in our algeraic identities can be evaluated at the points
p_{1,i},\,p_{2,i}
giving rise to numerical estimates.

Denote

\displaystyle{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}={h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{m(m+1)}}}\to 0\mbox{ \ (when $i\to\infty$).}

(2.95)

Let
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
with
|a_{i}|\leq{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}
. Then
1+|a_{i}|^{\frac{1}{m}}\preceq_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{m}}}
so,
by (
2.89
), (
2.94
), we have

\displaystyle(1+|a_{i}|^{\frac{1}{m}})^{m-1}|y_{k,i}|^{-1}\preceq_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m-1}{m}}}|y_{{{1}},i}|^{-1}\preceq_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}{h}_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m-1}{m}-\frac{m}{m+1}}}={\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}\mbox{ for }k={1},{2}.

(2.96)

Using the notations in (
2.71
) and the results in formula (
2.72
) and in formula (
2.14
) of Lemma
2.14
, we prove the following.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
k={1},{2}
and
a\in\mathbb{C}
with
|a|\leq{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}
and
i\gg 1
.

(i)

In
(
2.2
) (i)
, the series
P=F^{-\frac{1}{m}}
with respect to

converges strongly when
(x,y)
is set to

, and

P_{a,k}:=P|_{(x,y)=(a,y_{k,i})}=y^{-1}_{k,i}\Big(1+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1}\Big).

(2.97)

(ii)

In
(
2.2
) (ii)
, the series
y
with respect to
x,P
converges strongly when
(x,P)
is set to
