, and

Y_{a,k}:=y|_{(x,P)=(a,P_{a,k})}=y_{k,i}\Big(1+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1}\Big).

(2.98)

(iii)

In
(
2.14
)
,
the series
G
with respect to
x,P
converges strongly when
(x,P)
is set to
(a,P_{a,k})
, and

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }A_{a,k,\ell}:=\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=\ell}^{\infty}$}\tilde{c}_{{0}j}P_{a,k}^{j}\preceq_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}P_{a,k}^{\ell}\sim_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}y^{-\ell}_{{{1}},k}\mbox{ \ for \ }\ell\geq-n,

\displaystyle{\rm(b)\ }B_{a,k}:=G_{1}|_{(x,y)=(a,y_{k,i})}\preceq_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}{h}_{p_{{{1}},i},p_{{{2}},i}}y^{-(m-1)}_{k,i}\prec_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}y^{-(m-3)}_{{{1}},i}.

(2.99)

(iv)

The series
\big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\big)^{-1}
with respect to
x,P
converges strongly when
(x,P)
is set to
(a,P_{a,k})
, and

\displaystyle m\Big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\Big)^{-1}\Big|_{(x,P)=(a,P_{a,k})}=y^{-(m-1)}_{k,i}\Big(1+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1}\Big).

(2.100)

(v)

P_{{1}}:=P_{x_{{{1}},i},1}=P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})=P(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})=P_{x_{{{2}},i},2}
.

Proof.
From (
2.2
) (ii), we see that
{\textbf{{P}}}_{{\rm ign}}|_{(x,y)=(|a|,|y_{k,i}|)}
converges to
O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1}
by (
2.96
), thus (
2.97
) follows from
(
2.2
) (i). Similarly, we obtain (
2.98
)–(
2.100
) from (
2.2
), (
2.14
), (
2.2
).
This proves (i)–(iv).

To prove (v), we have
P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})^{-m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P==defineP}}}}{{=}}F(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})=F(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P==defineP}}}}{{=}}P(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})^{-m}
. Thus

P(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})

=\omega^{\prime}P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})
,

(2.101)

for some
m
-th root
\omega^{\prime}
of unity (which may depend on
i
). Assume there exists
j\leq m-3
such that

\tilde{c}_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{tcjne0}}}}{{=}}\tilde{c}_{{0}j}\neq 0
but
P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})^{j}\neq P(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})^{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{omega-p}}}}{{=}}\omega^{\prime j}P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})^{j}
.

(2.102)

Let
j_{0}\leq m-3
be the minimal such
j
. Then
\omega^{\prime j_{0}}\neq 1
and
|1-\omega^{\prime j_{0}}|>\delta
for some fixed
\delta>0
(since
\omega^{\prime j_{0}}
, which may though depend on
i
, is an
m
-th root of unity).
By (
2.14
) and Lemma
2.17
(iii), we have [note that
\tilde{c}_{{0},j_{0}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\neq 0}
is a number independent of
i
and
P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})\sim_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}y^{-1}_{{{1}},i}
by (
2.97
) ],

\displaystyle 0\ \ \ \ =\ \ \ \ \ G(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})-G(x_{{{2}},i},y_{{{2}},i})

\displaystyle\phantom{0}\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{P=Sm1+2+1},\,\eqref{PbP2}}}}{{=}}\tilde{c}_{{0},j_{0}}(1-\omega^{\prime j_{0}})P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})^{j_{0}}+A_{x_{{{1}},i},1,j_{0}+1}-A_{x_{{{2}},i},2,j_{0}+1}+B_{x_{{{1}},i},1}-B_{x_{{{2}},i},2}

\displaystyle\phantom{0}\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{PbP1},\,\eqref{PbP2}}}}{{\sim_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}P(x_{{{1}},i},y_{{{1}},i})^{j_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{PbP1}}}}{{\sim_{i\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}y^{-j_{0}}_{{{1}},i},

(2.103)

which is a contradiction. This proves the following crucial fact,

\omega^{\prime j}=1
for all
j\leq m-3
with
\tilde{c}_{j}\neq 0
.

(2.104)

In particular by (
2.72
) (ii),

\omega^{\prime m-4}=1
,
\omega^{\prime m-3}=1
, which implies that
\omega^{\prime}=1
. This proves (v) and the lemma.
\Box

Step 4 (conclusion).

Now we can continue our proof of Proposition
2.15
.
By (
2.40
), (
2.14
) and Lemma
2.17
(iv), (v), we have the following,
where the second equality follows from (
2.40
), (
2.71
) and Lemma
2.17
(iii), (v), the third equality follows from the fact that
\tilde{c}_{j}
’s are polynomials in
x
[the integration path is taken as
x(t):=x_{1,i}(1-t)+tx_{2,i},\ 0\leq t\leq 1{\scriptscriptstyle\,}
], the fourth follows from the fact that
the series there converges absolutely and uniformly when
|x|\leq h_{p_{1,i},p_{2,i}}
by Lemma
2.17
, and the last two equalities can be observed from (
2.2
) (see Remark
2.18
for the reason why we have the fifth equality),

\displaystyle 0=G(x_{1,i},y_{{{1}},i})-G(x_{2,i},y_{{{2}},i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ome-2},\,\eqref{Mememdnbrnrn}}}}{{=}}-\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\big(\tilde{c}_{j}(x_{2,i})-\tilde{c}_{j}(x_{1,i})\big)P_{{1}}^{j}

\displaystyle\phantom{0}=-\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\int_{x_{1,i}}^{x_{2,i}}\frac{d\tilde{c}_{j}}{dx}P_{{1}}^{j}dx=-\int_{x_{1,i}}^{x_{2,i}}\mbox{$\sum\limits_{j=-n}^{\infty}$}\frac{d\tilde{c}_{j}}{dx}P_{{1}}^{j}dx\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm Remark\ \ref{FifthEqrema}}}}{{=}}\int_{x_{1,i}}^{x_{2,i}}J_{0}\Big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\Big)^{-1}\,\Big|_{(x,P)=(x,P_{{1}})}dx\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle\phantom{0}\!\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{S-s---1}}}}{{=}}(x_{2,i}-x_{1,i})J_{0}m^{-1}y^{-(m-1)}_{1,i}\Big(1+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1}\Big)\neq 0,

(2.105)

which is a contradiction. This proves
Proposition
2.15
.

\Box

Remark 2.18
.

Note that the fifth equality of (
2.2
) should be understood in this way: first we regard
\big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\big)^{-1}
as a power series of
P
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}[x]
, i.e.,
J_{0}\big(\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\big)^{-1}=\sum_{j=-n}^{\infty}\frac{dc_{j}}{dx}P^{j}
by (
2.2
), then we set
P
to
P_{1}
(and keep
x
unchanged with
|x|\leq h_{p_{1,i},p_{2,i}}
), and then Lemma
2.17
(iv) shows that the series converges absolutely and uniformly, and so we have the fifth equality.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.
It follows immediately from Proposition
2.15
.
\Box

Remark 2.19
.

To prove Theorem
1.1
, we always need to make the whole use of the following three facts about
(F,G)
:

(F1)

F\in\mathbb{C}[x,y^{\pm 1}],

G\in\mathbb{C}[x]((y^{-1}))
;

(F2)

F,G
are locally holomorphic functions of
x,y
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and so
F,G\in\mathbb{C}[x,y]
;

(F3)

J(F,G)
is nonzero anywhere in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and so
J(F,G)\in\mathbb{C}_{\neq 0}
.

However,
some results may not require all of the above facts; for instance, our approach above shows that Theorem
1.2
holds for any pair
(F,G)

satisfying (F1) as long as the following holds [of course, when
(F,G)\notin\mathbb{C}[x,y]^{2}
, the map
\sigma
in (
1.1
) can be only defined on some subset of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and in case
G\notin\mathbb{C}[x,y^{\pm 1}]
, we need some extra condition on
G
so that it can be controlled somehow, cf. (
2.14
) ]:

(i)

J(F,G)\in\mathbb{C}_{\neq 0}
;

(ii)

the homogenous part of
F
with the highest degree contains only one term, i.e.,
y^{m}
with
m>1
;

(iii)

let
c_{\alpha}
be defined in (
2.40
), then,

\displaystyle{\rm gcd}\Big(m,m\alpha\,\Big|\,\alpha\in\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{Z},\alpha\geq\frac{3-m}{m},\,c_{\alpha}\neq 0\Big)=1,

(2.106)

where the symbol “ gcd ” denotes the greatest common divisor [cf. (
2.51
) and statements after (
2.2
), and noting that
\omega^{\prime}
appeared in (
2.2
) is some
m
-th root of unity].

In particular, Theorem
1.2
holds for the pair
(F,G)=(y^{m},y^{n}+cy^{k}+xy^{-m+1})
for any
m,n,k\in\mathbb{Z},

c\in\mathbb{C}_{\neq 0}
with
m>1
,
n>k\geq 3-m
and
{\rm gcd}(m,n,k)=1
, which can be also easily proven directly; in this case,
c_{\frac{n}{m}}=1,

c_{\frac{k}{m}}=c\neq 0.
If (
2.106
) does not hold, then the
m
-th root
\omega^{\prime}
of unity in (
2.2
)
is not necessarily equal to
1
[if we denote the left-hand side of (
2.106
) as
d
then the proof of Lemma
2.17
(v) shows that
\omega^{\prime d}=1
], and we can easily construct a counter example like this:
(F,G)=(y^{6},y^{4}+xy^{-5})
; in this case, we can choose, say,
(p_{1i},p_{2i})=\big((x_{1i},y_{1i}),(x_{2i},y_{2i})\big)=\big((1,i),(-1,-i)\big)\in V
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
\omega^{\prime}=-1
and Theorem
1.2
does not hold.

2.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
provided by Claudio Procesi

We thank Professor Claudio Procesi for providing us the following simple proof by using a little geometry (for your reference, we also present our original proof of surjectivity of
\pi_{1}
without using geometry in Appendix
A
).

Lemma 2.20
.

The set
V
defined in (
1.3
) is a smooth and closed algebraic surface in
\mathbb{C}^{4}=\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Proof.
The map
\phi:\mathbb{C}^{4}\to\mathbb{C}^{2},

\phi:\ (p_{1},p_{2})\mapsto\big(F(p_{1})-F(p_{2}),G(p_{1})-G(p_{2})\big),

has Jacobian matrix always of maximal rank so that
\phi^{-1}(0,0)=V\cup D
is a smooth closed surface where
D
is the diagonal which is closed. By any argument (the implicit functions theorem, or smoothness)
D
is also open in
\phi^{-1}(0,0)
and the lemma follows.

\Box

We need a simple estimate.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R},\ a,b>2
. If

b<2(a+b)^{\frac{m}{m+1}}
then
b<2(2a)^{m}
.

Proof.
We have
ab-a-b=(\frac{a}{2}-1)b+(\frac{b}{2}-1)a>0
, so

b<2(a+b)^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\implies b^{m+1}<2^{m+1}(a+b)^{m}<2^{m+1}(ab)^{m}\implies b<2(2a)^{m}.

[formula]

We apply this to
a=|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|,\ b=|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|
with
\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)\in V
and to the projection
\pi_{1}
[defined in (
1.5
) ] and
deduce the following.

Proposition 2.22
.

If
A\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
is bounded then also
\pi_{1}^{-1}(A)\subset V
is bounded.

Proof.
This follows from Lemma
2.21
, and Proposition
2.15
.
More precisely, fix any
{\textbf{{s}}}_{8}>{\textbf{{s}}}_{7}
(which is the number in Proposition
2.15
) such that for all
(x_{1},x_{2})\in A
,

a:=|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|<{\textbf{{s}}}_{8}
.

(2.107)

Let
(p_{1},p_{2})=\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)\in\pi_{1}^{-1}(A)
. We claim

\displaystyle b:=|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|<2(2{\textbf{{s}}}_{8})^{m}.

(2.108)

Assume conversely that
b\geq 2(2{\textbf{{s}}}_{8})^{m}
. Then
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{dp0p1}}}}{{\geq}}b\geq 2(2{\textbf{{s}}}_{8})^{m}>{\textbf{{s}}}_{7}
. Thus we can apply Proposition
2.15
to obtain,

b=|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|<2h_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{1},p_{2}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{dp0p1},\,\eqref{a-smalllThan},\,\eqref{b-is-smaller}}}}{{=}}2(a+b)^{\frac{m}{m+1}}.

(2.109)

Then by Lemma
2.21
,
b<2(2a)^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{a-smalllThan}}}}{{<}}2(2{\textbf{{s}}}_{8})^{m}
, which is a contradiction with the assumption. Thus we have (
2.108
) and the proposition.

\Box

As a consequence we have that the fibres of
\pi_{1}
, being compact affine algebraic varieties, are finite hence the closure, denoted
\overline{\pi_{1}(V)}
, of
\pi_{1}(V)
is a 2-dimensional subvariety of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
so it is
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.
Now we can proceed the proof of Theorem
1.3
as follows. We already see that
\pi_{1}
is proper and finite. Now take any point
q\in\mathbb{C}^{2}=\overline{\pi_{1}(V)}
. By the previous discussion there is a sequence of points
\pi_{1}(p_{i})
for
p_{i}\in V
with
\lim_{i\to\infty}\pi_{1}(p_{i})=q
. But the sequence
p_{i}
, by the previous proposition, is bounded and
V
is closed so we can extract a subsequence converging to some
p\in V
and
\pi_{1}(p)=q
.
\Box

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

First we recall after the proof of Lemma
2.9
that we have fixed, once and for all, the Jacobian pair
(F,G)
satisfying (
2.2
) and Lemma
2.9
.

The main purpose of this section is to prove that the projection
\pi_{1}:V\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
defined in (
1.5
) is in fact not surjective (which contradicts Theorem
1.3
and thus proves Theorem
1.1
). To help understanding, we first give some explanations.

Throughout the whole section, (though
V
may have some other properties) we only need to use the following three properties (C1)–(C3) satisfied by
V
[especially, (C2) will be frequently used], which do not necessarily depend on the fact that
V
arises from the Jacobian problem. As in (
1.3
), an element in
V
is usually denoted as
(p_{1},p_{2})=\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)
.

(C1)

V
is a nonempty closed subset of
{\mathbb{C}^{4}}
(by Lemma
2.20
);

(C2)

|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|=o(h_{p_{1},p_{2}})
when
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}:=|x_{1}|+|y_{1}|+|x_{2}|+|y_{2}|\to\infty
(by Theorem
1.2
);

(C3)

for each
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\in V
, there exists a neighborhood
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
of
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
such that for all
(p_{1},p_{2})=((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))\in V\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}
,
x_{1},y_{1}
are holomorphic functions of
x_{2},y_{2}
(by the local bijectivity of Keller maps).

Note that the local bijectivity of Keller maps also implies that
V
satisfies,

(C4)

there exists a non-constant polynomial
\theta(x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2})
such that
V\not\subset S_{\theta}
, where
S_{\theta}
is the set of zeros of the polynomial
\theta
,

\phantom{mama}S_{\theta}=\{((\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{y}_{1}),(\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{y}_{2}))\in\mathbb{C}^{4}\,|\,\theta(\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{y}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{y}_{2})=0\},

and further, for any
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\in V\backslash S_{\theta}
, there is some neighborhood
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
of
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
such that
for all
(p_{1},p_{2})=((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))\in V\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}
,
x_{1},x_{2}
are holomorphic functions of
y_{1},y_{2}
.

Remark 3.1
.

We would like to mention that if we change (C4) to the following slightly stronger form (C4)
′
, then the proof (attached in Appendix
C
) becomes very easy by using some geometry [since we already have (C4), if we change (C4)
′
to that the Jacobian matrix of
\pi_{1}
at any
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\in V\cap S_{\theta}
is of maximal rank, then we also get that
\pi_{1}
is not surjective]:

(C4)
′

for any
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\in V
, there is some neighborhood
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
of
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
such that
for all
(p_{1},p_{2})=((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2}))\in V\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}
,
x_{1},x_{2}
are holomorphic functions of
y_{1},y_{2}
.

Thus (C4) means that (C4)
′
holds for all elements in
V
except the zeros of
\theta
.
Because of Remark
3.1
, we do think that it is very reasonable to have that
\pi_{1}
is not surjective. However since we only have (C4) instead of (C4)
′
, we cannot treat
V
in a uniform way. This is why the proof in this section looks so complicated and why we have to consider case by case. As a matter of fact, in this section, we will try to avoid choosing elements of
V
which are in
S_{\theta}
[for example the element
(\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2})\in A_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}
satisfying (
3.6
) is an element not in
S_{\theta}
when
{\textbf{{k}}}\gg 1
].

3.1.
Two propositions

We want to build a proposition, namely, Proposition
3.7
, which will immediately imply another proposition, namely, Proposition
3.12
, that will give us a contradiction (thus proving Theorem
1.1
).

First we need to introduce some notations.
For any

k_{{1}},k_{{2}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, by Theorem
1.3
, the following is a nonempty compact subset of
V
,

\displaystyle A_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}=\big\{(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in V\ \big|\ |x_{{1}}|=k_{{1}},\,|x_{{2}}|=k_{{2}}\big\}\neq\emptyset.

(3.1)

Thus
the following is a well-defined function of
k_{{1}},k_{{2}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}={\rm max}\,\big\{|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|\ \big|\ (p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in A_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}\big\}.

(3.2)

We fix some choices of positive numbers
satisfying,

\displaystyle 1{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ll{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell_{0}:=\delta_{0}^{-1}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ll{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell_{1}:=\delta_{1}^{-1}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ll{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell_{2}:=\delta_{2}^{-1}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ll{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell:=\delta^{-1}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ll{\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\textbf{{k}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ll{\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}^{-1},\ \ \ \ \ \ell_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}.\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.3)

Remark 3.2
.

(i)

The choices may depend on the situation we encounter, and further, the choice of a number listed later may depend on all choices of numbers listed earlier; for instance, we may require that
\ell_{1}\gg\ell_{0}^{\ell_{0}},\,\ell_{2}\gg\ell_{1}^{\ell_{1}}
, etc. We always require
k
to be sufficiently larger than
{\textbf{{s}}}_{7}
,
with
{\textbf{{s}}}_{7}
being the number appeared in Proposition
2.15
:

\displaystyle{\textbf{{k}}}\gg{\textbf{{s}}}_{7}.

(3.4)

(ii)

We need to use the following convention: we can regard elements in (
3.3
) as parameters to be fixed upon our requirement in the situation we encounter. Sometimes we need to compare some number in (
3.3
) with other numbers, in this case, for convenience we may regard some element in (
3.3
) as a variable;
for instance, if we regard
\ell
as a variable, then we need to
regard
\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
as fixed numbers and
{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}
as
variables
such that we have [cf. (
2.90
) ],

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!1{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\sim_{\ell\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell_{0}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\sim_{\ell\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell_{1}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\sim_{\ell\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell_{2}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\prec_{\ell\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\ell{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\prec_{\ell\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\textbf{{k}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\prec_{\ell\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}^{-1}.\!\!\!\!\!

(3.5)

Whenever the parameter
{\textbf{{k}}}\gg\ell
is chosen, we fix an element
(\bar{p}_{{1}},\bar{p}_{{2}})=\big((\bar{x}_{{1}},\bar{y}_{{1}}),(\bar{x}_{{2}},\bar{y}_{{2}})\big)\in A_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}
satisfying

\displaystyle|\bar{x}_{{1}}|=|\bar{x}_{{2}}|={\textbf{{k}}},\ \ \ \ \ |\bar{x}_{{2}}+\bar{y}_{{2}}|=\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}.

(3.6)

Notation 3.3
.

(i)

For any
x_{{1}},x_{2},y_{{1}},y_{{2}}\in\mathbb{C}
, we denote,

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }X_{{1}}=\frac{x_{{1}}}{\bar{x}_{{1}}},\ \ \ \ \ {\rm(b)\ }X_{{2}}=\frac{x_{{2}}}{\bar{x}_{{2}}},\ \ \ \ \ {\rm(c)\ }Z=\frac{x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}}{\bar{x}_{{2}}+\bar{y}_{{2}}}.

(3.7)

(ii)

For any subset of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
, we always use the same symbol with an overbar to denote its closure in
\mathbb{C}^{4}
, that is, if
S
is a subset of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
,
then
\overline{S}
denotes its closure in
\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

The reason we define
(
3.7
) is in order that when
(p_{1},p_{2})=\big((x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{2},y_{2})\big)
is close to
(\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2})
we have that
X_{1},X_{2},Z
are close to
1
, which will make our arguments easier.

Definition 3.4
.

Denote by
S_{1}
the open subset of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
consisting of all elements

(x_{1},x_{2})
whose coordinates satisfy

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}<|X_{1}|<\ell_{2}^{4},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}<|X_{2}|<\ell_{2}^{4},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(iii)\ }\Big|2-\frac{1}{X_{1}}\Big|>\delta_{2}^{4}.

(3.8)

Set
S_{2}=\pi_{1}^{-1}(S_{1})
.

Note that
(X_{1},X_{2})=(1,1)
satisfies (
3.8
), thus by (
3.6
), (
3.7
),

(\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2})\in S_{2}
, i.e.,
S_{2}
is non-empty. Moreover,
S_{2}
is open in
V
and is a bounded set by Proposition
2.22
.

The reason we define
S_{2}
is to ensure that
A_{1},A_{2}
to be defined in Definition
3.6
are well-defined functions in
\overline{S}_{2}
.
The importance of condition (
3.8
) is
that we can obtain the following lemma, esp., (
3.5
) (iv), which is very crucial in obtaining Proposition
3.7
in case
V_{0}=V_{2}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

When
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\overline{S}_{2}\subset V
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}}\leq|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|\leq\ell_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }2\delta_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}}\leq h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\leq 2(\ell_{2}^{4}+\delta^{3}){\textbf{{k}}},

\displaystyle{\rm(iii)\ }x_{2}+y_{2}=x_{2}\Big(1+O(\delta)^{3}\Big),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(iv)\ }Z{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}X_{2}\Big(1+O(\delta)^{3}\Big)=X_{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}O(\delta)^{3},

\displaystyle{\rm(v)\ }(\delta_{2}^{4}{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}\delta^{2}){\textbf{{k}}}{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}|x_{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}y_{2}|{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}(\ell_{2}^{4}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\delta^{2}){\textbf{{k}}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(vi)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}{\scriptscriptstyle}-{\scriptscriptstyle}\delta^{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}|Z|{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}\ell_{2}^{4}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\delta^{2}.

(3.9)

Proof.
Formula (
3.5
) (i) follows from (
3.6
)–(
3.8
).
We have

\displaystyle 2\delta_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{equa-Case6-lemm}\,(i)}}}{{\leq}}|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{dp0p1}}}}{{\leq}}h_{p_{1},p_{2}}.

(3.10)

From this we obtain

\displaystyle h_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{1},p_{2}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{m+1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{msmsem999999}}}}{{\leq}}(2\delta_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}})^{-\frac{1}{m+1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{MSmde33333}}}}{{<}}\delta^{3}.

(3.11)

Thus

\displaystyle h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{dp0p1}}}}{{=}}|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|+|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{equa-Case6-lemm}\,(i),\,\eqref{mqp1234-2}}}}{{\leq}}2\ell_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}}+2h_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{1},p_{2}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{h-p-1-p-2===}}}}{{<}}2\ell_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}}+2\delta^{3}h_{p_{1},p_{2}}.

(3.12)

This with (
3.10
) proves (
3.5
) (ii). We have

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\Big|\frac{y_{2}}{x_{2}}\Big|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{mqp1234-2},\,\eqref{equa-Case6-lemm}\,(i)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{h_{{}_{\scriptstyle p_{1},p_{2}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}}{\delta_{2}^{4}{\textbf{{k}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{equa-Case6-lemm}\,(ii),\,\eqref{MSmde33333}}}}{{<}}\delta^{3},

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }x_{2}+y_{2}=x_{2}\Big(1+\frac{y_{2}}{x_{2}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{equa-Case6-lemm+1}\,(i)}}}{{=}}x_{2}\Big(1+O(\delta)^{3}\Big),

\displaystyle{\rm(iii)\ }Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{SimMMSMS}\,(c)}}}{{=}}\frac{x_{2}+y_{2}}{\bar{x}_{2}+\bar{y}_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{equa-Case6-lemm+1}\,(ii)}}}{{=}}\frac{x_{2}\Big(1+O(\delta)^{3}\Big)}{\bar{x}_{2}\Big(1+O(\delta)^{3}\Big)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{SimMMSMS}\,(b)}}}{{=}}X_{2}\Big(1+O(\delta)^{3}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{meme}\,(i)}}}{{=}}X_{2}+O(\delta)^{3}.

(3.13)

From the above we obtain (
3.5
) (iii), (iv). Then (
3.5
) (v) follows from (
3.5
) (i), (iii). Similarly by (
3.8
) (i), (
3.5
) (iv), we have (
3.5
) (vi).

\Box

Definition 3.6
.

Let
b_{\textbf{{k}}}>0
be the
number
to be defined in (
3.77
).
Denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{1}=\alpha_{0}X_{1},\ \ \ \ \alpha_{0}=1+\beta_{0}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \beta_{0}=-1+\delta^{3}b_{\textbf{{k}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{Akk-bkk}}}}{{>}}0.

(3.14)

For any
(p_{1},p_{2})\in\overline{S}_{2}
, we define [noting that (

) (iii) with (

)

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }A_{1}=X_{2}^{\ell_{0}}\Big(2-\frac{1}{\widetilde{X}_{1}}\Big),\ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }A_{2}=\frac{X_{2}^{\ell_{0}+1}}{Z}\Big(2-\frac{\widetilde{X}_{1}}{A_{1}^{2}}\Big).\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.15)

Now we can state our first proposition.

Proposition 3.7
.

There exist some
\kappa_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and some
\eta_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\neq 0}
such that we have the following.

Denote by
V_{1}
the subset of
V
consisting of all
elements
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)
whose coordinates
x_{1},x_{2},y_{2}
satisfy,

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }\displaystyle 1<{\scriptscriptstyle}(\kappa_{1}|x_{2}|)^{\eta_{1}}\leq\kappa_{2}|x_{1}|^{\eta_{2}}\leq(\kappa_{1}|x_{2}|)^{\eta_{3}}<\kappa_{3},\ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle{\rm(b)\ }\ell_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}:=\kappa_{4}|x_{2}|^{\eta_{4}}\cdot|x_{2}+y_{2}|+|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|\geq\kappa_{5}.

(3.16)

Denote by
V_{2}
the subset of
S_{2}
consisting of all
elements
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)
whose coordinates
x_{1},x_{2},y_{2}

satisfy
[
we will prove later on that
A_{2}

is
invertible in the set
W_{2}
to be defined in (
3.8
)
{\scriptscriptstyle\,}]
,

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }\displaystyle 1{\scriptscriptstyle}<{\scriptscriptstyle}|A_{1}|^{-\kappa_{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}|A_{2}|^{-1}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}|A_{1}|^{-\kappa_{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle}<{\scriptscriptstyle}\kappa_{3},\ \ \ \ \ {\rm(b)\ }\ell_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle}:={\scriptscriptstyle}|X_{2}|\cdot|A_{1}|^{\eta_{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}|x_{{2}}|{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}|x_{{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}y_{{2}}|{\scriptscriptstyle}\geq{\scriptscriptstyle}\kappa_{4},\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle{\rm(c)\ }(1-\delta)|X_{2}|\cdot|A_{1}|^{2+\delta_{0}}<|\widetilde{X}_{1}|<(1+\delta)|X_{2}|^{-1}\cdot|A_{1}|^{-\delta_{0}}.\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.17)

Then there exists
V_{0}
which is either equal to

or else equal to
V_{2}
such that

is a nonempty compact
subset of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
, and further when

, we have

\displaystyle x_{1},x_{2},x_{2}+y_{2}\neq 0.

(3.18)

Definition 3.8
.

Denote by
W_{1}
the subset of
V
consisting of all
elements
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=
\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)
whose coordinates

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }\displaystyle 1\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}(\kappa_{1}|x_{2}|)^{\eta_{1}}\leq\kappa_{2}|x_{1}|^{\eta_{2}}\leq(\kappa_{1}|x_{2}|)^{\eta_{3}}\leq\kappa_{3},\ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle{\rm(b)\ }\ell_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}:=\kappa_{4}|x_{2}|^{\eta_{4}}\cdot|x_{2}+y_{2}|+|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|\geq\kappa_{5}.

(3.19)

Denote by
W_{2}
the subset of
V
consisting of all
elements
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)
whose coordinates

satisfy,

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }\displaystyle 1{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}|A_{1}|^{-\kappa_{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}|A_{2}|^{-1}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}|A_{1}|^{-\kappa_{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle}\leq{\scriptscriptstyle}\kappa_{3},\,\ \ {\rm(b)\ }\ell_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle}:={\scriptscriptstyle}|X_{2}|\cdot|A_{1}|^{\eta_{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}|x_{{2}}|{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}|x_{{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}y_{{2}}|{\scriptscriptstyle}\geq{\scriptscriptstyle}\kappa_{4},\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle{\rm(c)\ }(1-\delta)|X_{2}|\cdot|A_{1}|^{2+\delta_{0}}\leq|\widetilde{X}_{1}|\leq(1+\delta)|X_{2}|^{-1}\cdot|A_{1}|^{-\delta_{0}}.\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.20)

Lemma 3.9
.

We have

{\rm(i)\ }\overline{V}_{1}\subseteq W_{1},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }\overline{V}_{2}\subseteq W_{2}\subseteq S_{2}.

(3.21)

Proof.
We prove the more complicated case (
3.21
) (ii).
Let
(p_{1},p_{2})\in W_{2}
.
Since
negative
powers appear
in (
3.8
) (a), we need to interpret
(
3.8
) (a) as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\kappa_{3}^{-1}\leq|A_{1}|^{\kappa_{2}}\leq|A_{2}|\leq|A_{1}|^{\kappa_{1}}\leq 1.\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.22)

In particular,
A_{1},A_{2}

are
invertible.
We have
\overline{V}_{2}\subset V
as
V
is closed by Lemma
2.20
.
We will see in (
3.119
), (
3.28
) that (
3.8
) implies (
3.8
). Hence,
W_{2}\subset S_{2}
.

Now let

(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2})=\big((\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1}),(\hat{x}_{2},\hat{y}_{2})\big)
be any element in
\overline{V}_{2}
. By definition of
\overline{V}_{2}
, there
exists
a sequence

(p_{1,i},p_{2,i})=\big((x_{1,i},y_{1,i}),(x_{2,i},y_{2,i})\big)\in V_{2}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}

converging to
(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2})
. Then (
3.8
) is satisfied by
(p_{1,i},p_{2,i})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}<|X_{1,i}|<\ell_{2}^{4},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}<|X_{2,i}|<\ell_{2}^{4},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(iii)\ }\Big|2-\frac{1}{X_{1,i}}\Big|>\delta_{2}^{4}.

(3.23)

By taking the limit of the sequence, we see from (
3.23
) that the hat versions

\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2}
of

X_{1},X_{2}
, which are limits of
X_{1,i},X_{2,i}
, satisfy the following,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}\leq|\hat{X}_{1}|\leq\ell_{2}^{4},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }\delta_{2}^{4}\leq|\hat{X}_{2}|\leq\ell_{2}^{4},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(iii)\ }\Big|2-\frac{1}{\hat{X}_{1}}\Big|\geq\delta_{2}^{4}.

(3.24)

Thus
\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2},\hat{Z}
[cf. (
3.5
) (iv) ]
are invertible, and

A_{1}

is
well-defined at
(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2})\in\overline{V}_{2}
, and further,
A_{1}
is invertible by
noting from (
3.14
) that the tilde version of
\hat{X}_{1}
is
\widetilde{\hat{X}}_{1}=\alpha_{0}\hat{X}_{1}
and
then noting from (
3.24
) (iii) that

\big|2-\frac{1}{{\widetilde{\hat{X}}}{}_{1}}\big|
is invertible. Thus

A_{2}

is
well-defined. Since (
3.7
) is satisfied by
(p_{1,i},p_{2,i})
,
by taking the limit of the sequence, we immediately see that (
3.8
) is satisfied by
(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2})
. By definition of
W_{2}
, we have
(\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{2})\in W_{2}
.
This completes the proof of (
3.21
) (ii).

\Box

Remark 3.10
.

(i)

We emphasis that
the last two terms of
\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}
in (
3.7
) (b) or (
3.7
) (b) play extremely important roles which guarantee the inequation (
3.26
) has solution
(q_{1},q_{2})
as required [cf. (
3.37
) ].

(ii)

When we prove Proposition
3.7
, the first thing we need to do is to prove
V_{0}\neq\emptyset
, which will be done by choosing some suitable element
(p_{1},p_{2})\in V_{0}
. We regard such an element as the “initial stage”. We will see from our arguments in this section that we observe the following important fact,

Fact 3.11
.

The “initial stage” controls globally the growths of
|x_{2}|,\,|x_{2}+y_{2}|
on the whole set
V_{0}
if we define
V_{0}
by (
3.7
).

(iii)

We use the strange way in (
3.15
) to define
A_{1},A_{2}
in order to satisfy Proposition
3.7
. More precisely,

(a)

Firstly, we will see in the proof of Lemma
3.29
that powers that appear in
(
3.15
) (i) play key roles.

(b)

Secondly, we need to prove
in Lemma
3.27
that
V_{2}\neq 0
by choosing the “initial stage”
(p_{1},p_{2})
, sufficiently close to
(\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2})
, satisfying (
3.7
), such that
all
elements in (
3.15
)
are
1+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1}
numbers (though they are not necessarily real numbers) and

X_{2},Z
have are positive numbers of
the following form (where
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}>0
is sufficiently small),

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }X_{2}=1,\ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }Z=1-\delta^{3}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}.\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.25)

(iv)

We would like to point out that because of our choice of the “initial stage” in (
3.25
) that
|Z|
is smaller than
|X_{2}|
, which is the key points to obtain a contradiction when the last inequality of (
3.8
) (a) becomes equality as there will exist an inconsistence in (
3.8
).

(v)

Note that we do not have any information about
y_{1}
in (
3.7
), (
3.7
). This is because we cannot put too many conditions in the definition of
V_{0}
. We can only use Theorem
1.2
to control
|y_{1}|
as long as we have some control on
|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|
.

Proposition 3.12
.

For any
(\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}})\in V_{0}
, in every neighborhood of
(\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}})
there exists
(q_{{1}},q_{{2}})=\big((\dot{x}_{{1}},\dot{y}_{{1}}),(\dot{x}_{{2}},\dot{y}_{{2}})\big)\in V_{0}

sufficiently close to
(\check{p}_{1},\check{p}_{2})
such that

\displaystyle\ell_{q_{{1}},q_{{2}}}>\ell_{\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}}}.

(3.26)

We will first use Proposition
3.7
to give a proof of Proposition
3.12
.

3.2.
Proof of Proposition
3.12
provided by Claudio Procesi and proof of Theorem
1.1

We thank Professor Claudio Procesi for providing us the material after (
3.36
) until the end of proof of Proposition

(for your reference we also present our original arguments in Appendix

).

Proof of Proposition
3.12
.
Let
(\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}})=\big((\check{x}_{{1}},\check{y}_{{1}}),(\check{x}_{{2}},\check{y}_{{2}})\big)\in V_{0}
.
Note that (
3.18
)
implies that
\check{x}_{{1}},\check{x}_{{2}},\check{x}_{{2}}+\check{y}_{{2}}\neq 0
.
We need to find an element
(q_{1},q_{2})\in V_{0}
which is sufficiently close to
(\check{p}_{1},\check{p}_{2})
. Thus we can assume
q_{1}=(\dot{x}_{1},\dot{y}_{1}),\,q_{2}=(\dot{x}_{2},\dot{y}_{2})
such that their coordinates have the following forms, for some sufficiently small
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}>0
, and
u,v,s,t\in\mathbb{C}
(for later convenient use, it is no harm to write the coordinate of
\dot{x}_{2}+\dot{y}_{2}
instead of
\dot{y}_{2}
),

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\dot{x}_{{1}}=\check{x}_{{1}}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}s{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}),\ \ \dot{y}_{1}=\check{y}_{{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}t{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1},\ \ \dot{x}_{{2}}=\check{x}_{{2}}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}),\ \ \dot{x}_{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\dot{y}_{{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}(\check{x}_{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\check{y}_{{2}})(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}v{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}).\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.27)

The local bijectivity of Keller maps says that for any
u,v\in\mathbb{C}
in a bounded set there always exists sufficiently small
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}>0
such that,
there exist
s,t\in\mathbb{C}

with
(q_{{1}},q_{{2}})\in V
, namely,
q_{1}\neq q_{2}
[which is guaranteed by the fact that
(q_{1},q_{2})
is sufficiently close to
(\check{p}_{1},\check{p}_{2})
and
\check{p}_{1}\neq\check{p}_{2}
] and

\big(F(q_{1}),G(q_{1})\big)=\big(F(q_{2}),G(q_{2})\big)
.

(3.28)

Since
F,G
are polynomials, we can always solve
s
(throughout the paper we do not need to use
t
so we omit the solution of
t
) from (
3.27
), (
3.28
) to obtain that
s
is a power series of
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}
such that each coefficient of
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}^{k}
is a homogenous polynomial of
u,v
with degree
k+1
for all
k\geq 0
; in particular, we can write
s
as the following form, for some

,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!s{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}s_{{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}(\tilde{a}u^{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\tilde{b}uv{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\tilde{c}v^{2}){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{2},\ \ \ s_{{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}au{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}bv,\ \ \ (a,b)\neq(0,0).\!\!\!\!

(3.29)

First note that since
(q_{1},q_{2})
is sufficiently close to
(\check{p}_{1},\check{p}_{2})
, we see that
(
3.8
), (
3.7
) (c) are
automatically satisfied by
(q_{1},q_{2})
in case

.
Further when (

) holds for
(q_{1},q_{2})
, we always have

\ell_{q_{1},q_{2}}>\ell_{\check{p}_{1},\check{p}_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ToSayas}\,(b)}}}{{\geq}}\kappa_{5}

(or respectively
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ToSayas+1}\,(b)}}}{{\geq}}\kappa_{4}
)
,

(3.30)

i.e., (

) (b) [or respectively (
3.7
) (b) ] automatically holds for

.
Thus in order for

and to satisfy (

), we only need to require (

) (a) [respectively (

) (a) ] and (

.

Observe from the first strict inequality of (

) (a) that

3.7
) (a) or (

) (a).
Therefore, we only need to consider the following two possible cases.

[

Then the same must hold for

(since

is infinitesimal), i.e.,

is automatically in

if (

) holds. Thus in this case we only need to consider one inequation, i.e.,
(
3.26
). Therefore we see that this case is easier than the case we encounter below (as we always have no need to worry about

appeared below).

Case 2:
Assume that, for

,
equality occurs in either the second or else the third inequality of

) (a)

[
or respectively
(
3.7
) (a)
{\scriptscriptstyle\,}]
.

Accordingly, we need to consider two inequations for

\displaystyle E_{1}:=\kappa_{2}\kappa_{1}^{-\eta_{1}}|\dot{x}_{1}|^{\eta_{2}}\cdot|\dot{x}_{2}|^{-\eta_{1}}-1\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{1++??+q0q1}}}}{{=}}|1+s{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}|^{\eta_{2}}\cdot|1+u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}|^{-\eta_{1}}-1\geq 0,

.

It may be worth mentioning that we have the following simple formulas,
for any

which are independent of

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }(1+a_{1}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{b_{1}}(1+a_{2}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{b_{2}}=1+(a_{1}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2}){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{2},

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\ }|1+a_{1}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}|^{b_{1}}=|1+a_{1}b_{1}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{2}|.

Using (

), (

of

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }E_{1}{\scriptscriptstyle}:={\scriptscriptstyle}\big|1+f(u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1},v{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})\big|-1\geq 0,

\displaystyle{\rm(ii)\,}E_{2}{\scriptstyle}:={\scriptstyle}\kappa_{{1}}^{\prime}\big|1{\scriptstyle}+g(u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1},v{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})\big|{\scriptstyle}+{\scriptstyle}|1{\scriptstyle}+{\scriptstyle}u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}|{\scriptstyle}+{\scriptstyle}\kappa^{\prime}_{{2}}|1{\scriptstyle}+{\scriptstyle}v{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}|{\scriptstyle}-{\scriptstyle}(\kappa^{\prime}_{{{1}}}{\scriptstyle}+{\scriptstyle}1+\kappa^{\prime}_{{2}}){\scriptstyle}>{\scriptstyle}0.\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.34)

For the case (

(3.35)

Thus by using (
3.2
), we can also write the two inequations we need to consider as the forms in (
3.2
).

First observe that for any

[recall Convention

(1) ], we have,

\displaystyle|1+\alpha{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}|=\sqrt{(1+\alpha_{\rm re}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{2}+(a_{\rm im}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{2}}=1+\alpha_{\rm re}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}+\frac{(\alpha_{\rm im})^{2}}{2}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}^{2}+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{3}.

Therefore the coefficient of the
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}

for some linear homogeneous form

in

so that the two forms take positive real values (and the proof of Proposition

Therefore we consider the later case and assume for instance that

.
Then

\displaystyle E_{2}=A(w){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}^{2}+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{3},\ \ \ A(w):=(\gamma w^{2})_{\rm re}+\frac{(w_{\rm im})^{2}}{2}+\kappa^{\prime}_{{2}}\frac{\big((\beta\alpha^{-1}w)_{\rm im}\big)^{2}}{2}.

(3.37)

Further

E_{1}
is either zero or of the form

,

Assume we have the later case as otherwise

\displaystyle E_{1}=(cw^{k})_{\rm re}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}^{k}+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1})^{k+1}.

(3.38)

If
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}=0
then we can easily choose

with

and

so that

and

by (

) and (

).

Thus assume
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}\neq 0
. We may take

with

for

with

.

Notice now that for any integer
h_{1}\geq 1
and any nonzero constant complex number
h_{2}
the two sets where
(h_{2}w^{h_{1}})_{\rm re}>0
respectively
(h_{2}w^{h_{1}})_{\rm im}>0
are two open sets of the circle
|w|=1
of arclength
\pi
.

Lemma 3.13
.

The set where
A(w)>0
is an open set of the circle
|w|=1
of arclength
\geq\pi+\delta
for some
\delta>0
so there is a nonempty open set of the circle
|w|=1

of arclength
\geq\delta
where both inequalities

A(w)>0
and
(cw^{k})_{\rm re}>0
hold.

Proof.
The set where
(\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}w^{2})_{\rm re}\leq 0
is formed by two opposite arcs of total arclength
\frac{\pi}{2}
so at least two of the
4
endpoints of these arcs, where
(\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}w^{2})_{\rm re}=0
, are different from
\pm 1
where
w_{\rm im}=0
, thus in a neighbourhood of these two points
(\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}w^{2})_{\rm re}+\frac{(w_{\rm im})^{2}}{2}>0
.

\Box

Note that we can always choose sufficiently small
{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}>0
such that
0<{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{1}\ll\delta
. This proves Proposition
3.12
.
\Box

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.
Now we use Proposition
3.12
to prove Theorem
1.1
. Proposition
3.12
(which says that the continuous function
\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}
does not have the maximal value on the nonempty compact set
V_{0}
) immediately gives a contradiction, which proves
the first statement of Theorem
1.1
. The second statement follows from
[
3
,
5
]
.

\Box

3.3.
Proof of Proposition
3.7

It remains to prove Proposition
3.7
.
We would like to mention that because we require
V_{0}
to be closed, it seems to us that in general there is no way to define a system of inequations (
3.7
) or (
3.7
) satisfying that
V_{0}
is closed. We need some “extra fact”.
By considering all possible different cases, each of which provides us some different “extra fact”, we will be able to achieve the goal.

Before proceeding our proof of Proposition
3.7
, let us give some further explanations. We aim to construct a nonempty subset
V_{0}
satisfying, say for example, (
3.7
).
To do this, usually we can start with some given element

(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})=\big((\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{y}_{1}),(\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{y}_{2})\big)\in V,

and of course we may choose some different
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
for a different case.
Look at the condition (
3.7
): if, for example, we take

\kappa_{0}=1,

\kappa_{1}=|\tilde{x}_{2}|^{-1}
,
\kappa_{2}=|\tilde{x}_{1}|^{-\eta_{2}},

then (
3.8
) (a) can be satisfied by
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
by choosing some suitable
\kappa_{3}>1.

We always need to choose
\kappa_{5}
in (
3.7
) (b) to be as big as possible
otherwise
V_{0}
can possibly contain some extra elements which we may be unable to control somehow. In order to do this, we should choose
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\in V
such that
|\tilde{x}_{2}+\tilde{y}_{2}|

is maximal (i.e.,
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{|\tilde{x}_{1}|,|\tilde{x}_{2}|}
). In this case we can choose
\kappa_{5}
to be

\beta_{0}:=\kappa_{4}|\tilde{x}_{2}|^{\eta_{4}}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{|\tilde{x}_{1}|,|\tilde{x}_{2}|}+|\tilde{x}_{2}|+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{|\tilde{x}_{1}|,|\tilde{x}_{2}|}.

In this way, we may have that
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\in\overline{V}_{0}
(the closure of
V_{0}
). However then, equality can occur in the
first inequality of (
3.8
) (a) and thus (
3.7
) (a) is not satisfied [i.e.,

(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\notin V_{0}
]. To avoid this, we have to enlarge
\kappa_{5}
[this is why we choose
\alpha
in (
3.3
) (b), respectively (
3.3
) (b), to be as in (
3.45
), respectively
(
3.63
) ]. Then
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
cannot be in
V_{0}
.
This means that we cannot choose the given element
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
as the “initial stage”.
We have to find some other element as the “initial stage”, which is the reason why we require some “extra fact”.

First we need a lemma.

Lemma 3.14
.

The
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}
defined in
(
3.2
)
is a strictly increasing function of
k_{{1}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
when
k_{{2}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is fixed, i.e.,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k^{\prime}_{{1}},k_{{2}}}>\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}\ \ \mbox{ if \ }k^{\prime}_{{1}}>k_{{1}}\geq 0,\ k_{{2}}\geq 0.

(3.39)

Consequently,
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{1},k_{2}}>0
for any
k_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\,k_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Proof.
For any
k^{\prime}_{{1}}>0
, let

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\beta\!\!

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\!\!{\rm max}\{\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}\,|\,0\leq k_{{1}}\leq k^{\prime}_{{1}}\}

(3.40)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{Ak=1}}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\!\!{\rm max}\big\{\,|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|\ \big|\ (p_{{1}},p_{{2}})=\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in V,\,0\leq|x_{{1}}|\leq k^{\prime}_{{1}},\,|x_{{2}}|=k_{{2}}\,\big\}\geq 0.

Assume
conversely that
there exists
k_{{1}}<k^{\prime}_{{1}}
with
k_{{1}}\geq 0
such that
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}=\beta
.
We will use the local bijectivity of Keller maps to obtain a contradiction.
Let

(\tilde{p}_{{1}},\tilde{p}_{{2}})=\big((\tilde{x}_{{1}},\tilde{y}_{{1}}),(\tilde{x}_{{2}},\tilde{y}_{{2}})\big)\in V
with
|\tilde{x}_{{1}}|=k_{{1}},\ \ |\tilde{x}_{{2}}|=k_{{2}},\ \ |\tilde{x}_{{2}}+\tilde{y}_{{2}}|=\beta
.

(3.41)

Using
x_{{2}},y_{{2}}
as local coordinates in
V
we choose
(q_{1},q_{2})\in V
sufficiently close to
(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})
, satisfying, for some
v\in\mathbb{C}
with
|v|
being sufficiently small,

\displaystyle q_{1}=(\dot{x}_{1},\dot{y}_{1}),\ \ q_{{2}}:=(\dot{x}_{2},\dot{y}_{2})=(\tilde{x}_{{2}},\tilde{y}_{{2}}+v),

(3.42)

such that

\displaystyle|\dot{x}_{{2}}+\dot{y}_{{2}}|=|\tilde{x}_{{2}}+\tilde{y}_{{2}}+v|>|\tilde{x}_{{2}}+\tilde{y}_{{2}}|.

(3.43)

Since
|\tilde{x}_{{1}}|=k_{{1}}<k^{\prime}_{{1}}
, and locally
x_{1}
is a holomorphic, hence a continuous function of
(x_{2},y_{2})
, we have
|\dot{x}_{{1}}|<k^{\prime}_{{1}}
when
|v|>0
is sufficiently small.
This means that we can choose
(q_{{1}},q_{{2}})\in V
with

|\dot{x}_{{1}}|<k^{\prime}_{{1}},\ \ \ |\dot{x}_{{2}}|=k_{{2}}
, but
|\dot{x}_{{2}}+\dot{y}_{{2}}|>\beta
,

which is a contradiction with the definition of
\beta
in (
3.40
). This proves the first assertion of the lemma. Then for any
k_{1}>0
,
k_{2}\geq 0
, we have

\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{1},k_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wePPPP1+}}}}{{>}}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\frac{k_{1}}{2},k_{2}}\geq 0,

proving the second.
\Box

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.
Now we proceed the proof of Proposition

3.7
case by case.

Remark 3.15
.

In the first five cases, we are able to define
\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}
such that we can choose the “initial stage”,
which controls the growths of
|x_{2}|,\,|x_{2}+y_{2}|
as
mentioned in Fact
3.11
, to guarantee that

|x_{2}+y_{2}|
can grow faster (or decrease slower) than
|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|
when
|x_{1}|
or
|x_{2}|
goes to infinity [see for example (
3.100
) ];
thus by Theorem
1.2
,
|x_{2}|
cannot go too far from the correspondent value of the “initial stage”,
which allow us to choose some
\kappa_{4}
in (
3.7
) (a) so that for any element in
\overline{V}_{0}
, equality cannot occur in the last inequality of
(
3.8
) (a).

Case 1: Assume
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{1},k_{2}}
is not a weakly increasing function of
k_{2}
, i.e.,
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}^{\prime}}>\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}
for some
k_{{1}}>0,\,k_{{2}}>k_{{2}}^{\prime}>0
.

This provides us the following “extra fact” (
3.45
):

(1)

There exists a
\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle(k_{{2}}^{-1}k^{\prime}_{{2}})^{\delta}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}^{\prime}}>\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}},

(3.44)

since strict inequality holds when
\delta=0
.

(2)

Once such
\delta
is fixed, there is a constant
{\textbf{{k}}}_{0}
so that for
{\textbf{{k}}}>{\textbf{{k}}}_{0}
we have:

\displaystyle\alpha:=(k_{{2}}^{-1}k^{\prime}_{{2}})^{\delta}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}^{\prime}}+(k^{\prime}_{{2}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}^{\prime}}){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}>\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}+(k_{{2}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}.

(3.45)

We will see in (
3.54
) what is the role the above “extra fact” plays.

We define
V_{1}=V_{1}({\textbf{{k}}})
, depending on the parameter
{\textbf{{k}}}>{\textbf{{k}}}_{0}
, to be the subset of
V
consisting of all
elements
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})\!=\!\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)
such that its coordinates
x_{1},x_{2},y_{2}
satisfy,

\displaystyle{\rm(a)\ }1<(k_{2}|x_{2}|^{-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}\leq k_{1}^{-1}|x_{1}|\leq(k_{2}|x_{2}|^{-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}<{\textbf{{k}}}^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}},

\displaystyle{\rm(b)\ }\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}:=(k_{{2}}^{-1}|x_{{2}}|)^{\delta}|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|+(|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\geq\alpha.

(3.46)

Remark 3.16
.

(i)

As mentioned in Remark
3.2
(ii), we treat
k
as a parameter which will be fixed upon our requirement in the course of the proof.

(ii)

We put
{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}
in (
3.3
) (b) in order for the element
(|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}
to be sufficiently smaller than
\alpha
when (
3.3
) holds.

(iii)

The reason we put the factor
(k_{{2}}^{-1}|x_{{2}}|)^{\delta}
in (
3.3
) (b) is to guarantee that when the last strict inequality of (
3.3
) (a) becomes an equality,
|x_{2}|
will decrease, but
|x_{2}+y_{2}|
will grow.

(iv)

Note that the number in the last term of (
3.3
) (a) cannot be too big, otherwise for an element in
\overline{V}_{1}
, when the last strict inequality of (
3.3
) (a) becomes equality,
|x_{1}|
will grow faster than
|x_{2}+y_{2}|

and then we cannot apply Theorem
1.2
.

First, multiplying (
3.3
) (b) by
k
and re-denoting
{\textbf{{k}}}\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}
as
\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}
, we see that
(
3.3
) can be rewritten in the form (
3.7
).

Remark 3.17
.

For a point in
V_{1}
, by (
3.3
) (a), we have

1<(k_{2}|x_{2}|^{-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}
,
(k_{2}|x_{2}|^{-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}<{\textbf{{k}}}^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}},
which implies (ii) below,

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }k_{1}\leq|x_{1}|<{\textbf{{k}}}^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}k_{1},\ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }k_{2}{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}<|x_{2}|<k_{2}.

(3.47)

where (i) is obtained from the part
1<k_{1}^{-1}|x_{1}|<{\textbf{{k}}}^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}
in (
3.3
) (a).

Now we prove that
V_{1}({\textbf{{k}}})\neq\emptyset
(for all
{\textbf{{k}}}>{\textbf{{k}}}_{0}
) by choosing some suitable “initial stage”
(\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}})
mentioned in Remark
3.10
(ii).
By hypothesis,
k_{{2}}k^{\prime-1}_{{2}}>1
, so denote

\displaystyle\check{k}_{{1}}:=(k_{{2}}k^{\prime-1}_{{2}})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}k_{{1}}>k_{{1}}.

(3.48)

By definition, there exists,

(\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}})=\big((\check{x}_{{1}},\check{y}_{{1}}),(\check{x}_{{2}},\check{y}_{{2}})\big)\in V
with
|\check{x}_{{1}}|=\check{k}_{{1}},\ \ |\check{x}_{{2}}|=k^{\prime}_{{2}}
,
|\check{x}_{{2}}+\check{y}_{{2}}|=\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\check{k}_{{1}},k^{\prime}_{{2}}}
.

(3.49)

When
(p_{1},p_{2})
is set to
(\check{p}_{1},\check{p}_{2})
, we denote the middle three terms in (
3.3
) (a) by
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
respectively.
Then we have the following three facts:

(a)

T_{1}:=(k_{2}|\check{x}_{2}|^{-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GEXiSTS}}}}{{=}}(k_{2}k_{2}^{\prime-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{kPPP}}}}{{>}}1

by (
3.49
) and (
3.48
) respectively;

(b)

T_{2}:=k_{1}^{-1}|\check{x}_{1}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GEXiSTS},\,\eqref{kPPP}}}}{{=}}(k_{2}k_{2}^{\prime-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm(a)}}}{{=}}T_{1}
by (
3.49
), (
3.48
) and (a) respectively;

(c)

T_{3}:=(k_{2}|\check{x}_{2}|^{-1})^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm(a)}}}{{=}}T_{1}^{1+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}>T_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm(b)}}}{{=}}T_{2}
by (a) and (b) respectively.

Thus we obtain

\displaystyle 1<T_{1}=T_{2}<T_{3}<{\textbf{{k}}},

(3.50)

i.e., (
3.3
) (a) is satisfied by
(\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}})
.

As for (
3.3
) (b), recall that, from Lemma
3.14
, we have
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\check{k}_{{1}},k^{\prime}_{{2}}}>\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k^{\prime}_{{2}}}
.
Using (
3.49
) and the definition of
\alpha
given by (
3.45
), we obtain:

\displaystyle(k_{{2}}^{-1}|\check{x}_{{2}}|)^{\delta}|\check{x}_{{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}\check{y}_{{2}}|{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}(|\check{x}_{{2}}|{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}|\check{x}_{{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}\check{y}_{{2}}|){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GEXiSTS}}}}{{=}}(k_{{2}}^{-1}k^{\prime}_{{2}})^{\delta}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\check{k}_{{1}},k^{\prime}_{{2}}}+(k^{\prime}_{{2}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\check{k}_{{1}},k^{\prime}_{{2}}}){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}

\displaystyle\phantom{(k_{{2}}^{-1}|\check{x}_{{2}}|)^{\delta}|\check{x}_{{2}}+\check{y}_{{2}}|+(|\check{x}_{{2}}|+|\check{x}_{{2}}+\check{y}_{{2}}|){\textbf{{k}}}^{-4}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wePPPP1+},\,\eqref{kPPP}}}}{{>}}(k_{{2}}^{-1}k^{\prime}_{{2}})^{\delta}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k^{\prime}_{{2}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}(k^{\prime}_{{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle\!}+{\scriptscriptstyle\!}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k^{\prime}_{{2}}}){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GMSMSM0999+1}}}}{{=}}\alpha,

(3.51)

namely, (
3.3
) (b) is satisfied by
(\check{p}_{{1}},\check{p}_{{2}})
. Hence we see that the “initial stage”
(\check{p}_{1},\check{p}_{2})
is in
V_{1}
. We take
V_{0}=V_{1}\neq\emptyset
.

Observe that

\lim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\to\infty}{\textbf{{k}}}^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}=1.

So we can take
{\textbf{{k}}}_{0}
so that if
{\textbf{{k}}}>{\textbf{{k}}}_{0}
we have
{\textbf{{k}}}^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}<2
. Thus by (
3.47
), we obtain, when
{\textbf{{k}}}>{\textbf{{k}}}_{0}
and
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})\!=\!\big((x_{{1}},y_{{1}}),(x_{{2}},y_{{2}})\big)\in V_{1}
,

(i)
k_{1}\leq|x_{1}|\leq k_{1}{\textbf{{k}}}^{{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-2}}<2k_{1}
, (ii)
k_{2}{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\leq|x_{2}|\leq k_{2}
.

(3.52)

In particular,
we have
x_{1},x_{2}\neq 0
.
Assume (
3.3
) holds with
x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}=0
.
Then (
3.3
) (b) shows
that
|x_{2}|\geq\alpha{\textbf{{k}}}>k_{2}
by (
3.45
) (when
k
is sufficiently larger than
k
as
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}>0
), a contradiction with (
3.52
) (2). Thus we have (
3.18
) [we do not have
A_{3}
in the present case]. Further,
V_{0}
is bounded by (
3.52
) and Proposition
2.22
.

Now we want to prove that
V_{0}
is closed. Thus we let
(p_{{1}},p_{{2}})\in\overline{V}_{0}
.
By Lemma
3.9
, first assume that the first strict inequality of
(
3.3
) (a) becomes an equality for
(p_{1},p_{2})
. Then we immediately obtain that
|x_{{2}}|=k_{{2}},\,|x_{{1}}|=k_{{1}}
.

For this point thus we must have

\displaystyle\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GMSMSMSMS}\,(ii)}}}{{=}}|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|+(k_{2}+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GMSMSMSMS}\,(ii)}}}{{\geq}}\alpha.

(3.53)

By definition (
3.2
), we have
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}\geq|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|,

which gives

\displaystyle\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}+(k_{{2}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{Ak=1},\,\eqref{ell-p1p2===}}}}{{\geq}}\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ell-p1p2===}}}}{{\geq}}\alpha.

(3.54)

The above is a contradiction with the “extra fact” (
3.45
), which proves that the first strict inequality of
(
3.3
) (a) is satisfied by
(p_{1},p_{2})
.

This also implies that the second and third inequalities cannot be both equalities.

Continuing, now assume the last strict inequality of (
3.3
) (a) becomes an equality for
(p_{1},p_{2})
, i.e.,
k_{2}|x_{2}|^{-1}={\textbf{{k}}}
,
we denote

\displaystyle\beta=\frac{1}{2}(k_{{2}}^{-1}k^{\prime}_{{2}})^{\delta}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}^{\prime}}>0.

(3.55)

Then by (
3.45
),

2\beta<\alpha,\ \text{ \ or equivalently, \ }\ \alpha-\beta>\beta
.

(3.56)

First assume

\alpha-(|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\geq\beta
.

(3.57)

Then by (
3.3
) (b),

|x_{2}+y_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GMSMSMSMS}\,(b),\,\eqref{fir-ASS}}}}{{\geq}}\beta(k_{2}^{-1}|x_{2}|)^{-\delta}=\beta{\textbf{{k}}}^{\delta}
.

(3.58)

We want to prove that (
3.58
) holds in general. Thus assume now we do not have (
3.57
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\alpha-(|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|){\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}<\beta,\ \ \ \implies\ \ \ \ |x_{2}|+|x_{2}+y_{2}|>(\alpha-\beta){\textbf{{k}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{b-alpha}}}}{{>}}\beta{\textbf{{k}}},\ \ \ \implies

\displaystyle|x_{2}+y_{2}|>\beta{\textbf{{k}}}-|x_{2}|=\beta{\textbf{{k}}}-k_{2}{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}.

(3.59)

Again for
k
sufficiently large we have
\beta{\textbf{{k}}}-k_{2}{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}>\beta{\textbf{{k}}}^{\delta}
, thus (
3.58
) holds in general.

Now then
|y_{2}|\geq|x_{2}+y_{2}|-|x_{2}|\geq\beta{\textbf{{k}}}^{\delta}-k_{2}{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\geq\frac{\beta}{2}{\textbf{{k}}}^{\delta}
, which,
together with the definition of
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}
in (
1.4
) implies

\displaystyle h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{dp0p1}}}}{{\geq}}|y_{{2}}|>\frac{\beta}{2}{\textbf{{k}}}^{\delta}.

(3.60)

Note from (
3.3
), Remark
3.2
(ii) and Remark
3.16
that we can choose
k
as large as we wish. When
k
tends to infinity also
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\to\infty
by (
3.55
), (
3.60
). Thus we can apply Theorem
1.2
to obtain that
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\sim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}|x_{1}|+|x_{2}|
, which with (
3.52
) shows that
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\sim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}1
, a contradiction with (
3.60
)
[by (
3.52
), (
3.58
) and by Remark
3.23
(to be given later), one can see why we can get a contradiction]. This proves that (
3.3
) is satisfied by
(p_{1},p_{2})
. By definition,
(p_{1},p_{2})\in V_{0}
. Thus
\overline{V}_{0}=V_{0}
.

The above shows that with
V_{1}=V_{1}({\textbf{{k}}})
being defined by (
3.3
), we have, for
k
sufficiently large, Proposition
3.7
. Case 1 is now completed.

Thus from now on, we can assume that
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}
is a weakly increasing function of
k_{{2}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
when
k_{{1}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is fixed, i.e.,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}\geq\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}^{\prime}}\ \ \mbox{ if \ }k_{{1}}>0,\ k_{{2}}>k_{{2}}^{\prime}>0.

(3.61)

Case 2: Assume

\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}\geq\bar{k}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}
for some
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\,\bar{k},k_{{1}},k_{{2}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
with
\bar{k}>1,\,\delta^{\prime}<\frac{1}{m}.

By choosing
\delta^{\prime\prime}
with
\delta^{\prime}<\delta^{\prime\prime}<\frac{1}{m}
and by Lemma
3.14
[or (
3.39
) ], we have

\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{wePPPP1+}}}}{{>}}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}\geq\bar{k}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}
.

(3.62)

This, as in Case 1, provides us the following “extra fact” with
\delta,{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}
satisfying (

) [here we require that

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\alpha:=(\bar{k}k_{{2}})^{-(1+\delta)}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}+(\bar{k}k_{{2}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}){\textbf{{n}}}^{-1}>k_{{2}}^{-(1+\delta)}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}+(k_{{2}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}){\textbf{{n}}}^{-1}.\!\!\!

(3.63)

We define

to be the subset of

\displaystyle{\rm(b)\ }\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}:=\frac{|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|}{\,|x_{{2}}|^{1+\delta}\,}+(|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|){\textbf{{n}}}^{-1}\geq\alpha.

As in Case 1,

).

Remark 3.18
.

The reason we put the power

, which is bigger than

Now we prove that

with

\displaystyle\phantom{{\rm(a)\ }1}<T_{3}:=(k_{2}^{-1}|\check{x}_{2}|)^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GEXiSTS+}}}}{{=}}\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}}<{\textbf{{k}}}^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}+{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}},

\displaystyle{\rm(b)\ }\frac{|\check{x}_{2}+\check{y}_{2}|}{|\check{x}_{2}|^{1+\delta}}+(|\check{x}_{2}|+|\check{x}_{2}+\check{y}_{2}|){\textbf{{n}}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GEXiSTS+}}}}{{=}}\frac{\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}}{(\bar{k}k_{{2}})^{1+\delta}}+(\bar{k}k_{{2}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}){\textbf{{n}}}^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{GMSMSM0999}}}}{{=}}\alpha,

i.e., (

) is satisfied by

.
We take

.

Exactly similar to Case 1, we can obtain that

Hence from now on, we can assume

\displaystyle\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{\bar{k}^{1+\delta^{\prime}}k_{{1}},\bar{k}k_{{2}}}<\bar{k}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{{1}},k_{{2}}}\mbox{ for any $\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\,\bar{k},k_{{1}},k_{{2}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$ with $\bar{k}>1,\,\delta^{\prime}<\frac{1}{m}.$}

Then we can prove

Lemma 3.19
.

For any
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, we have
\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{1},k_{2}}>k_{2}.

Proof.

\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k^{\prime}_{1},k_{2}}<\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k_{1},k_{2}}\leq k_{2}

\alpha:=\frac{\gamma_{k^{\prime}_{1},k_{2}}}{k_{2}}<1
. By (
3.72

\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}}k^{\prime}_{1},{\textbf{{k}}}k_{2}}<{\textbf{{k}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\,}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{k^{\prime}_{1},k_{2}}={\textbf{{k}}}k_{2}\alpha
for all
{\textbf{{k}}}\gg 1
. Let

\displaystyle(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\in V
with

,

,
|\tilde{x}_{2}+\tilde{y}_{2}|=\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}}k^{\prime}_{1},{\textbf{{k}}}k_{2}}<{\textbf{{k}}}k_{2}\alpha
.

(3.73)

By Proposition
2.15
or (

h_{\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{dp0p1},\,\eqref{mqp1234-2}}}}{{\sim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}|\tilde{x}_{1}|+|\tilde{x}_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{meleTTT}}}}{{\sim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}{\textbf{{k}}}
,

(3.74)

but then

|\tilde{y}_{2}|\geq|\tilde{x}_{2}|-|\tilde{x}_{2}+\tilde{y}_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{meleTTT}}}}{{>}}(1-\alpha){\textbf{{k}}}k_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{thahahaha}}}}{{>}}h_{{}_{\scriptstyle\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}}\mbox{ \ (when ${\textbf{{k}}}\gg 1$)},

(3.75)

which is a contradiction with
(
2.82
).
\Box

Recall that we fix
some choices of positive numbers

\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\dot{x}_{{1}}=\bar{x}_{{1}}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}s{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}),\ \ \dot{y}_{1}=\bar{y}_{{1}}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}t{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}},\ \ \dot{x}_{{2}}=\bar{x}_{{2}}(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}),\ \ \dot{x}_{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\dot{y}_{{2}}{\scriptscriptstyle}={\scriptscriptstyle}(\bar{x}_{2}{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}\bar{y}_{{2}})(1{\scriptscriptstyle}+{\scriptscriptstyle}v{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}).\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.76)

and as in (

\displaystyle s=-a_{\textbf{{k}}}u+b_{\textbf{{k}}}v+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1},\ \ \ (a_{\textbf{{k}}},b_{\textbf{{k}}})\neq(0,0).

We will see that the numbers

play crucial roles in our definition of

. First we prove

Lemma 3.20
.

We have
a_{\textbf{{k}}}\geq 0,\,b_{\textbf{{k}}}\geq 0
.

Proof.
Assume

a_{{\textbf{{k}}}\,\rm im}\neq 0
or
a_{{\textbf{{k}}}\,\rm re}<0
[cf. Convention

(1) ]. Then
we can choose

u,v\in\mathbb{C}

with

being sufficiently large to guarantee

3.78
) (iii) holds; if

to guarantee

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }u_{\rm re}<0,\ \ {\rm(ii)\ }v_{\rm re}>0,\ \mbox{ and such that \ }{\rm(iii)\ }s_{\rm re}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{@suc2hthat=4}}}}{{=}}(-a_{\textbf{{k}}}u+b_{\textbf{{k}}}v)_{\rm re}+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{1}<0.

Then by (

) and (

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }0<k_{{1}}:=|\dot{x}_{{1}}|={\textbf{{k}}}|1+s{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{u-v====}\,(iii)}}}{{<}}{\textbf{{k}}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }0<k_{{2}}:=|\dot{x}_{{2}}|={\textbf{{k}}}|1+u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}|<{\textbf{{k}}},

This means that

and

with the following [where the first inequality follows from definition (

,

\Box

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be such that
\delta^{\prime}>\log({\textbf{{k}}})^{-1}

[
where

is the natural logarithmic function

\displaystyle{\textbf{{k}}}<\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}<(1+\delta^{\prime}){\textbf{{k}}}.

3.19
. By (

), (

), when

, we have

\displaystyle|\bar{y}_{2}|\geq|\bar{x}_{2}+\bar{y}_{2}|-|\bar{x}_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{TaKa}}}}{{\geq}}\delta^{\prime}{\textbf{{k}}}\geq\log({\textbf{{k}}})^{-1}{\textbf{{k}}}\succ{\textbf{{k}}}^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\sim h_{{}_{\scriptstyle\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2}}}^{{}^{\scriptstyle\frac{m}{m+1}}},

(3.82)

a contradiction with (
2.82
).
\Box

Case 3: Assume for any
{\textbf{{s}}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}

We define

to be the subset of

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }1{\scriptstyle}<{\scriptstyle}\big({\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}|x_{{2}}|\big)^{1+\frac{1}{m}-\delta^{\prime}-{\textbf{{n}}}^{-1}}{\scriptstyle}\leq{\scriptstyle}{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}|x_{{1}}|{\scriptstyle}\leq{\scriptstyle}({\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}|x_{{2}}|)^{1+\frac{1}{m}{\scriptstyle}}<{\scriptstyle}{\textbf{{n}}}^{1+\frac{1}{m}},\ \ \

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm(ii)\ }\ell_{p_{1},p_{2}}:=\frac{\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}^{-1}|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|}{\big({\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}|x_{{2}}|\big)^{1+\delta^{\prime}}}{\scriptstyle}+(|x_{{2}}|+|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}^{3}\geq{\scriptstyle}1{\scriptstyle}+{\scriptstyle}{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}^{2}.

Similar to (
3.3
), we put the power

, which is bigger than

, in the denominator of (

) (ii)
is to ensure that

will grows faster than

when equality occurs in the last strict inequality of

We will prove

and

is bounded such that

which is obtained by Theorem

.
Then (

) (ii) with (

) gives

\displaystyle|x_{2}+y_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{NSoOP}\,(ii),\,\eqref{gAuaua}}}}{{>}}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}\big({\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}|x_{{2}}|\big)^{1+\delta^{\prime}},

(3.86)

which is also satisfied by any element in

by Lemma

In particular, we have
(
3.18
).

Now we prove that
V_{1}\neq\emptyset
by choosing some suitable “initial stage”

mentioned in Remark

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }1<|1+u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}|^{1+\frac{1}{m}-\delta^{\prime}-{\textbf{{n}}}^{-1}}\leq|1+s{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}|\leq|1+u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}|^{1+\frac{1}{m}}<{\textbf{{n}}}^{1+\frac{1}{m}},

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm(ii)\ }\frac{\Big|1+v{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}\Big|}{|1+u{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}|^{1+\delta^{\prime}}}+O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}})^{3}\geq 1+{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}^{2}.

(3.87)

\displaystyle u=1,\ \ \ v=\frac{a_{\textbf{{k}}}+1+\frac{1}{m}-\delta^{\prime}}{b_{\textbf{{k}}}},

,

(3.88)

where the last equation is obtained from (

).
Then the coefficients of

in the middle three terms of (

) (i) are respectively

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{m}-\delta^{\prime}-{\textbf{{n}}}^{-1},

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{m}-\delta^{\prime}
,
\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{m}
,

(3.89)

i.e.,
all inequalities in (
3.3
) (i) are strict inequalities
by recalling from notation (

) that we have complete freedom in choosing

with

Further, the coefficient of

\displaystyle v-(1+\delta^{\prime})u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{LetF3rst}}}}{{=}}\frac{a_{\textbf{{k}}}+1+\frac{1}{m}-\delta^{\prime}}{b_{\textbf{{k}}}}-(1+\delta^{\prime}),

which is bigger than

.
We take

.

Next we let

.
By Lemma

, first assume the first strict inequality of (

) (i) becomes equality for

. Then
|x_{1}|=|x_{2}|={\textbf{{k}}}
, but by

Now assume the last strict inequality of (

Then we obtain, when

[using (

(3.91)

but by

\displaystyle|x_{{2}}+y_{{2}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{x2+ya2}}}}{{\succeq_{{\textbf{{n}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}|x_{2}|^{1+\delta^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ansnen0000}}}}{{\sim_{{\textbf{{n}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}{\textbf{{n}}}^{1+\delta^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ansnen0000}}}}{{\succ_{{\textbf{{n}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}|x_{{2}}|.

(3.93)

By (
3.91
) and (

{h}_{p_{{1}},p_{{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{dp0p1},\,\eqref{mqp1234-2}}}}{{\sim_{{\textbf{{n}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}|x_{{1}}|+|x_{{2}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{ansnen0000}}}}{{\preceq_{{\textbf{{n}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}{\textbf{{n}}}^{1+\frac{1}{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{y2=a=a=a}}}}{{\prec_{{\textbf{{n}}}\scriptscriptstyle\,}}}|y_{{2}}|^{\frac{m+1}{m}}

a contradiction with

) [by (

This proves that (

) is satisfied by

, and so

Proposition

3.7
holds. Case 3 is now completed.

Hence from now on, we can assume

\displaystyle b_{\textbf{{k}}}\geq 1+\frac{1}{m}+a_{\textbf{{k}}}
when
{\textbf{{k}}}\gg\ell
.

Before continuing the proof, at this point it may be worth stating the following remark.

Remark 3.23
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a parameter such that
a\gg 1
. If for some
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
independent of

with
\delta^{\prime}<\frac{1}{m}
, we have
|x_{1}|\preceq_{a\scriptscriptstyle\,}a^{1+\delta^{\prime}}
, and further either
|x_{2}|\succeq_{a\scriptscriptstyle\,}a
or

|x_{2}+y_{2}|\succeq_{a\scriptscriptstyle\,}a

[thus
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\succeq_{a\scriptscriptstyle\,}|x_{2}|\succeq_{a\scriptscriptstyle\,}a
or
h_{p_{1},p_{2}}\succeq_{a\scriptscriptstyle\,}|x_{2}+y_{2}|\succeq_{a\scriptscriptstyle\,}a
], then (
3.102
) has in fact proved that
|x_{2}|
cannot differ from

|x_{2}+y_{2}|
by a factor which is independent of
a
[in case
|x_{2}|>|x_{2}+y_{2}|
, we use the inequation
|y_{2}|\geq|x_{2}|-|x_{2}+y_{2}|
in (
3.102
) ].

Now assume the first strict inequality of (
3.3
) (i),
becomes equality for
(p_{1},p_{2})
.
Then
|x_{1}|=|x_{2}|={\textbf{{k}}}
, but by
(
3.99
),
|x_{2}+y_{2}|>\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}
, a contradiction with definition (
3.2
).

This proves that (
3.3
) is satisfied by
(p_{1},p_{2})
, i.e.,
(p_{1},p_{2})\in V_{0}
, and so
V_{0}
is closed.
Proposition

3.7
holds. Case 4 is now completed.

Hence from now on, we assume

(1-\delta^{\prime})b_{\textbf{{k}}}\leq 1+a_{\textbf{{k}}}\mbox{ for any fixed sufficiently small $\delta^{\prime}>0$ and all ${\textbf{{k}}}\gg\ell$}.

(3.103)

This with (
3.95
) shows that
a_{\textbf{{k}}}\geq\frac{(1-\delta^{\prime})(1+\frac{1}{m})}{\delta^{\prime}}
. Since
\delta^{\prime}>0
is arbitrarily sufficiently small number,
we see that
a_{\textbf{{k}}}>0
(thus also
b_{\textbf{{k}}}>0
) is unbounded, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\to\infty}a_{\textbf{{k}}}=\lim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\to\infty}b_{\textbf{{k}}}=\infty,\mbox{ \ \ \ and }\ \ \ \lim_{{\textbf{{k}}}\to\infty}\frac{a_{\textbf{{k}}}}{b_{\textbf{{k}}}}=1.

(3.104)

.
Thus up to
O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{0})^{1}
,
we obtain,

\displaystyle|x_{2}+y_{2}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{LetNSoOP-add}\,(ii),\,(iii),\,\eqref{Thehehehdxb}\,(ii)}}}{{>}}(1-\delta)^{\lambda_{2}^{\prime-1}}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{WecanWSAS}\,(iii)}}}{{>}}(1-\delta)^{\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{2}}}\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}

\displaystyle\phantom{|x_{2}+y_{2}|}\ \ \ \ \ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm Lemma\ \ref{Anlemmmmmm}}}}{{\geq}}(1-\delta)^{\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{2}}}{\textbf{{k}}}=\Big(1-\frac{\delta}{1+\lambda_{2}}+O(\delta)^{2}\Big){\scriptscriptstyle\,}{\textbf{{k}}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

(3.113)

\displaystyle\phantom{|x_{2}+y_{2}|}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{amdnen333}}}}{{=}}\ \ \ \Big(\big(1+\alpha\delta+O(\delta)^{2}\big)(1-\delta)\Big){\textbf{{k}}}=\big(1+\alpha\delta+O(\delta)^{2}\big)|x_{2}|\mbox{ \ [up to $O({\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{0})^{1}$]},\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!

where

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{\lambda_{2}}{1+\lambda_{2}}>0
, which is a number independent of
k
.

(3.114)

By (
3.111
), (
3.3
), using arguments as in (
3.100
), (
3.102
),
we obtain a contradiction (cf. Remark
3.23
).

Now assume the first strict inequality of
(
3.3
) (i) becomes equality for
(p_{1},p_{2})
.
Then
|x_{1}|=|x_{2}|={\textbf{{k}}}
. Thus
|x_{2}+y_{2}|\leq\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|}=\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}
by definition (
3.2
). We have

\displaystyle 1+({\textbf{{k}}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{0}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{LetNSoOP-add}\,(iii)}}}{{<}}\kappa_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{}^{\scriptstyle\rm\eqref{LetNSoOP-add}\,(ii)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}^{-1}|x_{2}+y_{2}|}{({\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}|x_{2}|)^{\lambda_{2}^{\prime-1}(1+{{\small\textbf{{\footnotesize$\scriptstyle\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}}_{0})}}+(|x_{2}|+|x_{2}+y_{2}|){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{1+({\textbf{{k}}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{0}^{2}}\ \ \ \ \,=\ \ \ \ \mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}^{-1}|x_{2}+y_{2}|+({\textbf{{k}}}+|x_{2}+y_{2}|){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{0}^{2}\leq 1+({\textbf{{k}}}+\mbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\!}$\Large$\gamma$}_{{\textbf{{k}}},{\textbf{{k}}}}){\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}_{0}^{2},

(3.115)

which is a contradiction.

This proves that (
3.3
) is satisfied by
(p_{1},p_{2})
, i.e.,
(p_{1},p_{2})\in V_{0}
, and so
V_{0}
is closed.
Proposition

3.7
holds. Case 5 is now completed.

Thus from now on we assume that Case 5 does not occur.

Case 6: The remaining case.

In this case, unfortunately, we are not able to use (
3.7
) to define
V_{0}
. We have to define
V_{0}
in a more complicated way. The reason is below:

Remark 3.24
.

Because
b_{\textbf{{k}}}
is too big [by (
3.95
) ], we are unable to choose suitable ‘‘initial stage’’
(q_{1},q_{2})
mentioned in Remark
3.10
(ii) such that
u,v,s
satisfy (
3.77
), and
(q_{1},q_{2})
, defined in
(
3.76
), satisfies (
3.3
) if we define
V_{0}
as in (
3.3
). To see this, observe that in order for (
3.3
) (ii) to hold, we
have to choose
v
to be bigger than
u
but then (
3.77
) shows that
s
may possibly be too big, which
implies that
|x_{1}|
may grow too fast by Fact
3.11
. Since we do not have any information
about
y_{1}
in our definition of
V_{0}
, when
|x_{1}|\gg|x_{2}|
we can not apply Theorem
1.2

to obtain a contradiction if the last strict inequality of (
3.3
) (i) becomes equality for an element in
\overline{V}_{0}
.
Therefore in order to able to obtain a contradiction,
we have to find some other way to control the growth of
|x_{1}|
.

Recall notations (
3.3
), (
3.7
) and (
3.15
) and Remark
3.2
; in particular, we will frequently use the fact that
0<{\textbf{{\footnotesize$\mathbf{\mathcal{E}}$}}}\ll{\textbf{{k}}}^{-1}\ll\delta=\ell^{-1}\ll\delta_{2}=\ell_{2}^{-1}\ll\delta_{1}=\ell_{1}^{-1}\ll\delta_{0}=\ell_{0}^{-1}\ll 1
.

Definition 3.25
.

Recall that
A_{1},A_{2}
are the following functions defined in (

\displaystyle{\rm(i)\ }A_{1}=X_{2}^{\ell_{0}}\Big(2-\frac{1}{\widetilde{X}_{1}}\Big),\ \ \ \ \ \ {\rm(ii)\ }A_{2}=\frac{X_{2}^{\ell_{0}+1}}{Z}\Big(2-\frac{\widetilde{X}_{1}}{A_{1}^{2}}\Big).\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!
